Chow polynomials of rank-uniform labeled posets
Basile Coron, Luis Ferroni, Shiyue Li

Abstract
. We introduce and develop the theory of UMEL-shellable posets. These are posets equipped with an edge-lexicographical labeling satisfying certain uniformity and monotonicity properties. This framework encompasses classical families of combinatorial geometries, including uniform matroids, projective and affine geometries, braid matroids of type A and B, and all Dowling geometries. It also comprises all rank-uniform supersolvable lattices, and therefore also all rank-uniform distributive lattices. Our main result establishes real-rootedness phenomena for the Chow polynomials, the augmented Chow polynomials, and the chain polynomials associated with those posets, thus making simultaneous progress towards conjectures by Ferroni–Schröter, Huh–Stevens, and Athanasiadis–Kalampogia-Evangelinou. In the special case of lattices of flats of matroids, the (augmented) Chow polynomials coincide with the Hilbert–Poincaré series of the Chow ring associated to the smooth and generally noncompact toric varieties of the (augmented) Bergman fan of the matroid, whereas the chain polynomial encodes the Hilbert–Poincaré series of the Stanley–Reisner ring of the Bergman complex of the matroid. Therefore, these real-rootedness results are tightly linked to the study of these algebro-geometric structures in matroid theory.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Real-rootedness in algebraic and geometric combinatorics

Over the past few years, the algebraic study of matroids and geometric lattices has been transformed by a surge of ideas bridging combinatorics, geometry, and algebra. What began as the search for combinatorial proofs of positivity conjectures has grown into a rich and interconnected theory, informed by deep insights from algebraic and polyhedral geometry (see
[
3
,
37
,
27
]
). Yet, this progress has also given rise to new challenges. Several algebraically defined polynomial invariants of matroids have been recently conjectured to have only real roots, bringing analytic tools into play.

Among
positivity
phenomena for polynomials with nonnegative coefficients, real-rootedness stands as the strongest: it is equivalent to the Toeplitz matrix of a polynomial being totally nonnegative (
[
18
, Theorem 1.3]
) and, by Newton’s inequalities
[
51
, Theorem 2]
, it implies the log-concavity and the unimodality of the coefficients. Many fascinating connections of this property with the notions of
\gamma
-positivity and Koszulity of rings (see, e.g.,
[
47
, Section 4]
,
[
29
, Section 5.4]
) have also motivated new avenues of research.

While many algebraic and geometric invariants of matroids are known to be log-concave, unimodal, or
\gamma
-positive, the property of real-rootedness remains more elusive. A central motivating question of the present work is the following conjecture of Ferroni and Schröter
[
31
, Conjecture 8.18]
.

Conjecture 1.1

The Chow polynomial of a matroid has only nonpositive real roots.

This conjecture, and a counterpart for
augmented
Chow polynomials, has been independently proposed by Huh and Stevens
[
53
, Conjecture 4.3.3]
.

The Chow polynomial of
\mathrm{M}
is originally defined as the Hilbert–Poincaré series of the Chow ring of
\mathrm{M}
. The Chow ring of
\mathrm{M}
was introduced by Feichtner and Yuzvinsky
[
28
]
and played a crucial role in the resolution of the Heron–Rota–Welsh conjecture by Adiprasito, Huh, and Katz
[
1
]
. When the matroid
\mathrm{M}
is represented by a hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{A}
of size
n
, the Chow ring of
\mathrm{M}
is the Chow ring of the wonderful variety, or the wonderful compactification of the complement of
\mathcal{A}
, introduced by De Concini and Procesi in
[
22
]
. The wonderful variety is a subvariety of the smooth and projective permutohedral toric variety
X_{\underline{A}_{n}}
, and its Chow ring admits a set of generators given by base-point-free divisor classes pulled back from
X_{\underline{A}_{n}}
. As a graded vector space over any infinite field, the Chow ring has two combinatorial monomial bases, given by nested sets in the lattice of flats
[
28
]
and nested matroid quotients
[
7
]
. The augmented Chow polynomial is defined analogously as the Hilbert–Poincaré series of the augmented Chow ring of
\mathrm{M}
; this ring was introduced by Braden, Huh, Matherne, Proudfoot, and Wang
[
12
]
and was instrumental in the proof of the Dowling–Wilson top-heavy conjecture by the same authors
[
11
]
. When the matroid
\mathrm{M}
is represented by a hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{A}
, the augmented Chow ring of
\mathrm{M}
is the Chow ring of an iterated blowup, called the augmented wonderful variety, of the matroid Schubert variety of
\mathrm{M}
introduced by Ardila and Boocher
[
2
]
. The wonderful variety of
\mathcal{A}
appears as a divisor inside the augmented wonderful variety of
\mathcal{A}
. The augmented wonderful variety is a subvariety of the smooth and projective stellahedral toric variety
X_{A_{n}}
, and its Chow ring admits a set of generators given by base-point-free divisor classes pulled back from
X_{A_{n}}

[
26
]
. The two monomial bases of the Chow ring of
\mathrm{M}
immediately generalize to augmented monomial bases of the augmented Chow ring of
\mathrm{M}
. Therefore, the (augmented) Chow polynomial can be viewed as the generating polynomials of these monomial bases. Despite the rich structural results about these varieties, we do not know algebro-geometric explanations for why their Chow rings exhibit real-rooted Hilbert–Poincaré series in extensive computations. In contrast, closely related projective spaces, Grassmannians and flag varieties are well-known to have Chow rings with Hilbert–Poincaré series possessing non-real roots.

In recent work of Ferroni, Matherne, and Vecchi
[
30
]
an alternative combinatorial definition of Chow and augmented Chow polynomials of graded posets is introduced, circumventing the necessity of taking the Hilbert–Poincaré series of a ring. When the poset equals
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{M})
, the lattice of flats of a matroid
\mathrm{M}
, these notions agree with the Hilbert–Poincaré series of the Chow ring and the augmented Chow ring of
\mathrm{M}
(we recapitulate the key definitions in Section
2.4
). In a nutshell, the Chow polynomial
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
of a graded bounded poset
\mathrm{P}
is the value that the inverse of the reduced characteristic function takes at
[\widehat{0},\widehat{1}]
in the incidence algebra of
\mathrm{P}
(here
\widehat{0}
and
\widehat{1}
denote the minimum and maximum elements of
\mathrm{P}
). The augmented Chow polynomial
\mathrm{P}
, denoted
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
, is the Chow polynomial of the poset obtained by adding a minimum below
\mathrm{P}
. This poset-theoretic perspective enables us to formulate our first main result.

Theorem 1.2

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a UMEL-shellable poset.
Then the following statements hold:

(1)

The Chow polynomial
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
only has real and nonpositive roots.

(2)

The augmented Chow polynomial
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
only has real and nonpositive roots.

The precise definition of a
UMEL-shellable
poset is presented in Section
3
. This new class of posets is a subclass of EL-shellable posets
[
10
]
, i.e., posets admitting an edge-lexicographic labeling (for more details, see the recapitulation in Section
2
). The letter ‘U’ in the acronym UMEL stands for posets being
rank-uniform
: this means that for any two elements
s,t\in\mathrm{P}
of the same rank, the upper intervals
[s,\widehat{1}]
and
[t,\widehat{1}]
have the same Whitney numbers of the second kind, i.e., the same number of rank
j
elements for each
j
. When
\mathrm{P}
is a geometric lattice these posets were studied in great detail by Brylawski
1
1

1

Brylawski
[
20
, p. 223]
uses the term
upper combinatorially uniform
(or plainly uniform) for the posets we call rank-uniform.

[
20
]
. The letter ‘M’ stands for a technical monotonicity condition that the edge-lexicographic labeling must satisfy. These conditions altogether provide us with new recurrence relations for Chow polynomials, which we employ along with the machinery of interlacing families to deduce several real-rootedness results. The proof of the above theorem also enables us to uncover strong interlacing phenomena between the roots of the foregoing polynomials. For UMEL-shellable posets, the first of the three statements below settles
[
29
, Conjecture 5.7]
, whereas the second and the third statements settle a private conjecture of Li (see also
8.5
),

Theorem 1.3

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a UMEL-shellable poset. The following statements hold:

(1)

The roots of
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
and
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
interlace.

(2)

For an atom
a
of
\mathrm{P}
, the roots of
\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]}(x)
and
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)

interlace.

(3)

For an atom
a
of
\mathrm{P}
, the roots of
\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]}(x)
and
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)

interlace.

Several interesting classes of matroids are covered by the preceding results (see Theorem
1.8
below). Furthermore, the above result also makes progress towards a more general conjecture on the real-rootedness of Chow polynomials appearing in
[
30
, Conjecture 1.5]
by Ferroni, Matherne, and Vecchi. That conjecture proposes that the Chow polynomial of any Cohen–Macaulay poset is real-rooted. The posets considered in this article are Cohen–Macaulay as a consequence of being EL-shellable.

Let us denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathrm{M})
the set of all proper nonempty flats of
\mathrm{M}
. Since the poset
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{M})
is a geometric lattice, the order complex
\Delta(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathrm{M}))
, often called the
Bergman complex of
\mathrm{M}
, displays many desirable combinatorial and topological features—for example it is shellable and thus Cohen–Macaulay.
The
f
-vector of the Bergman complex of
\mathrm{M}
encodes the number of flags of flats in
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathrm{M})
according to size, and so its generating function, the
f
-polynomial of the complex, is often called the
chain polynomial
of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathrm{M})
. A recent conjecture by Athanasiadis and Kalampogia-Evangelinou
[
6
, Conjecture 1.2]
proposes that the chain polynomial of a geometric lattice is a real-rooted polynomial. This conjecture can be recast in terms of
h
-polynomials (which are linear transformations of
f
-polynomials) as follows.

Conjecture 1.4

The
h
-polynomial of the order complex of the lattice of flats (or, equivalently, the Bergman complex) of a matroid has only nonpositive real roots.

The
h
-polynomial of a
d
-dimensional simplicial complex
\Delta
will be denoted by
h(\Delta;y)
.
It can also be defined by

\operatorname{Hilb}(S/I_{\Delta};y)\coloneqq\sum_{i\geqslant 0}\dim(S/I_{\Delta})_{i}\,y^{i}=\frac{h(\Delta;y)}{(1-y)^{d+1}},

where
S/I_{\Delta}
is the Stanley-Reisner ring of
\Delta
over any field, and
\operatorname{Hilb}(\,\cdot\,;y)
stands for Hilbert–Poincaré series. The Chow ring
\underline{\mathrm{CH}}(\mathrm{M})
can be presented as a quotient of the Stanley–Reisner ring of
\Delta(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathrm{M}))
. However, no general relationship between the Hilbert–Poincaré series of these rings is known—except for the special case of uniform matroids (see
[
34
, Conjecture 6.2]
and
[
29
, Corollary 3.17]
).

Unlike the case of augmented Chow polynomials for which real-rootedness is conjectured to be true, the augmented counterpart of Conjecture
1.4
is known to be false. The
augmented Bergman complex
, introduced in
[
12
]
, does not in general have a real-rooted
h
-polynomial—in fact, by results of Athanasiadis and Ferroni
[
5
, Section 4]
even unimodality fails for this polynomial.

The following is another main result. It provides a proof of Conjecture
1.4
for the class of UMEL-shellable posets.

Theorem 1.5

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a UMEL-shellable poset. The
h
-polynomial of the order complex
\Delta(\mathrm{P})
of
\mathrm{P}
only has real and nonpositive roots.

The proof of this result is very similar in spirit to the proof of Theorem
1.2
. As is hinted from the above discussion, it is unexpected that one can prove instances of both Conjectures
1.1
and
1.4
using essentially the same framework. In fact, before carrying out the proof of Theorem
1.2
in Section
5
, we present the proof of Theorem
1.5
in Section
4
. The reason is that the ideas used to prove the latter are instrumental to prove the former, which is more technical.

Specializing Theorem
1.5
to the matroids listed below, in Theorem
1.8
, yields the real-rootedness of the
h
-polynomial of the Bergman complex of several families of matroids. This retrieves recent results appearing in the literature, especially in the work of Athanasiadis and Kalampogia-Evangelinou
[
6
]
and Brändén and Saud-Maia-Leite
[
14
,
13
]
(see below for a historical remark).

1.2.
Rank selection, truncations, and duality

If
n
is a positive integer, we denote
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. For a graded bounded poset
\mathrm{P}
of rank
r
and a subset
S\subseteq[r-1]
the
rank-selected
poset
\mathrm{P}_{S}
is the subposet of
\mathrm{P}
induced on the subset
\{x\in\mathrm{P}:\operatorname{rk}(x)\in S\cup\{0,r\}\}
. In the special case in which
S=[r-2]
, the poset
\mathrm{P}_{S}
is customarily called the
truncation
of
\mathrm{P}
, and we denote it by
\operatorname{Tr}(\mathrm{P})
. When
\mathrm{P}
is the lattice of flats of a matroid
\mathrm{M}
, the truncation of
\mathrm{P}
agrees with the lattice of flats of the matroid truncation of
\mathrm{M}
.

In Proposition
7.1
we show that truncations preserve the property of being a UMEL-shellable poset. In the framework of Chow polynomials this is especially interesting because of the tight connection between truncations and poset duality:

\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}^{*}}(x)=\mathrm{H}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathrm{P})}(x)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}^{*}}(x)=\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x).

Here
\mathrm{P}^{*}
stands for the poset that results from reversing all the comparability relations of
\mathrm{P}
. These identities were one of the main results proved by Brändén and Vecchi in
[
16
, Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 4.2]
, and allow us to get for free many results about duals of UMEL-shellable posets. Nonetheless we give very simple and self-contained proofs in this paper (see Lemma
2.4
).

It is natural to ask whether all rank selections preserve the property of being UMEL-shellable. While the question remains open (see Question
7.3
below), we nevertheless show that Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.5
are true for any rank selection of a UMEL-shellable poset. The following is among the most general statements we prove in the present article.

Theorem 1.6

Let
\mathrm{P}
be UMEL-shellable poset and let
\mathrm{P}_{S}
be a rank-selected subposet of
\mathrm{P}
. Then the following holds:

(1)

The Chow polynomials
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}}(x)
and
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}^{*}}(x)
have nonpositive real roots.

(2)

The augmented Chow polynomial
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}}(x)
has nonpositive real roots.

(3)

The roots of the polynomials
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}}(x)
and
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}}(x)
interlace.

(4)

The
h
-polynomial of the order complex
\Delta(\mathrm{P}_{S})
has nonpositive real roots.

Some ideas employed to prove this result are modifications and generalizations of tools used by Athanasiadis, Dovroupoulous, and Kalampogia-Evangelinou
[
4
]
.

1.3.
Supersolvable lattices

The class of supersolvable lattices, introduced by Stanley
[
49
]
, serve as a far-reaching generalization of both lattices of subgroups of supersolvable finite groups, as well as intersection lattices of fiber-type arrangements (e.g. braid arrangements of type A and B, see
[
43
]
), among many other examples. A supersolvable lattice can be simply defined as a lattice containing a maximal chain of modular elements. From this definition one can see that the class of supersolvable lattices encompasses the class of modular lattices and thus in particular all distributive lattices. A classical result of McNamara
[
41
]
establishes that any choice of a maximal chain of modular elements in a lattice induces an explicit EL-labeling on that lattice, and thus supersolvable lattices are EL-shellable.

In this article we focus on rank-uniform supersolvable lattices. In the special case of geometric lattices, these posets were studied by Damiani, D’Antona, and Regonatti
[
21
]
. The class of supersolvable rank-uniform geometric lattices contains remarkable families of geometric lattices, prominent examples being all braid matroids of type A and type B, and more generally all Dowling geometries
[
23
,
24
]
. Our main result concerning rank-uniform supersolvable lattices is the following.

Theorem 1.7

Every rank-uniform supersolvable lattice is UMEL-shellable.

The above is a central step towards deducing the specializations to matroids discussed below.

1.4.
Specializations to matroids

As we show in this paper, many famous classes of matroids have lattices of flats that are UMEL-shellable, and are thus covered by our framework. The following is a specialization of Theorems
1.2
,
1.5
, and
1.6
to matroids.

Theorem 1.8

The following classes of matroids have real-rooted Chow polynomials, augmented Chow polynomials, and
h
-polynomials of their Bergman complexes.

•

Uniform matroids.

•

Projective geometries and
q
-niform matroids.

•

Affine geometries and their truncations.

•

Braid matroids of type A and their truncations.

•

Braid matroids of type B and their truncations.

•

Dowling geometries and their truncations (generalizations of the preceding two families).

•

Supersolvable rank-uniform geometric lattices and their truncations (generalizations of the preceding three families).

In particular, Conjectures
1.1
and
1.4
hold for these matroids.

As explained earlier, a motivating feature of the class of posets addressed in the present article is that it enables a simultaneous approach for both Conjecture
1.1
and Conjecture
1.4
. It is worth mentioning at this point the work by Brändén and Saud-Maia-Leite
[
14
,
13
]
and by Brändén and Vecchi
[
15
]
(the latter written concurrently and independently of the present manuscript). The main results in these works establish, respectively, the real-rootedness of the chain polynomial and the Chow polynomial of certain posets called
TN-posets
. A TN-poset is a
lower rank-uniform
poset; instead of requiring that
upper
intervals of the same rank have the same Whitney numbers of the second kind, they require it for the
lower
intervals. The acronym TN stands for totally nonnegative: it is required that a certain matrix constructed from these lower rank-uniform posets is totally nonnegative. The classes of TN-posets and UMEL-shellable posets appear to share many interesting features (after passing to the dual). It is unclear, however, how these two classes compare. In the special case of geometric lattices, we conjecture (see Conjecture
8.6
) that all TN-geometric lattices are in fact UMEL-shellable, whereas the converse is easily seen to be false (partition lattices are UMEL-shellable but not TN-posets).

Some of the classes of matroids listed in the preceding theorem were approached in the literature recently by different sets of authors.

•

Uniform matroids were proved to have real-rooted augmented Chow polynomials in
[
29
, Theorem 1.10]
. The real-rootedness of the Chow polynomial of uniform matroids is the main result of Brändén and Vecchi in
[
16
]
; subsequently a different proof was provided by Hoster and Stump in
[
36
]
. The real-rootedness of the
h
-polynomial of the Bergman complex of a uniform matroid follows from results of Brenti and Welker
[
19
]
.

•

Projective geometries and their truncations (also known as
q
-niform matroids
[
46
]
or subspace lattices) were proved to have a real-rooted chain polynomial in work of Athanasiadis and Kalampogia-Evangelinou
[
6
, Proposition 3.1]
.

•

Projective and affine geometries were proved to have real-rooted Chow and augmented Chow polynomials by Brändén–Vecchi
[
15
, Section 6]
and real-rooted
h
-polynomials of their Bergman complexes by Brändén and Saud-Maia-Leite
[
14
, Theorem 3.7]
.

•

Braid matroids of type A and B and in fact all Dowling geometries were proved to have real-rooted augmented Chow polynomials by Brändén and Vecchi in
[
15
]
. The real-rootedness of the
h
-polynomial of the Bergman complex for braid matroids of type A and B was proved by Athanasiadis and Kalampogia-Evangelinou in
[
6
, Section 3]
; an alternative proof for these two classes of matroids and Dowling lattices was provided by Brändén and Saud-Maia-Leite
[
14
, Theorem 4.1]
.

The real-rootedness of the Chow polynomial and the augmented Chow polynomial for type A and type B braid matroids (and more generally, for Dowling geometries) was the original new result that motivated the discovery of UMEL-shellable posets
2
2

2

After we communicated our discovery, Brändén–Vecchi noticed an alternative proof of this fact, which is now contained in
[
15
, Section 6]
).
. The Chow and the augmented Chow polynomial of the type A braid matroid have been in the center of intensive study in recent years, see
[
32
,
29
,
30
,
40
,
54
,
25
,
38
]
.

1.5.
Structure of the paper

In
Section

2
, we recapitulate the main notions on EL-shellable posets, face and flag enumeration on posets, as well as expressions of the polynomials in question using these enumerations. We also include properties of interlacing polynomials, a key tool to prove real-rootedness of single variate polynomials. In
Section

3
, we present and develop the framework of UMEL-shellable posets, along with technical properties that follow from the definitions. In
Section

4
and
Section

5
, we introduce refinements of
h
-polynomials of order complexes and
\gamma
-polynomials of Chow polynomials into interlacing sequences of polynomials, using the recursive structures given by face and flag enumerations. These two sections contain the proofs of the main real-rootedness results, Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.5
. In
Section

6
, we show that rank-uniform supersolvable lattices are UMEL-shellable, which enables us to conclude real-rootedness results for those posets. In combination with the results on rank selection, mainly Theorem
1.6
), presented in
Section

7
, we establish the real-rootedness of the foregoing families of polynomials for the matroids listed in Theorem
1.8
. We finish the article with a list of open problems and conjectures in Section
8
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Posets and edge labelings

Let
(\mathrm{P},\leqslant)
be a finite graded bounded poset with rank function
\operatorname{rk}\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. We denote by
\widehat{1}
the upper bound of
\mathrm{P}
and by
\widehat{0}
its lower bound. We denote by
\mathrm{At}(\mathrm{P})
the set of elements of rank
1
, called the atoms of
\mathrm{P}
. For all
s\leqslant t\in\mathrm{P}
, the interval between
s
and
t
is denoted by

[s,t]\coloneqq\{u\in\mathrm{P}\mid s\leqslant u\leqslant t\}.

If
s<t\in\mathrm{P}
are such that no
u\in\mathrm{P}
satisfies
s<u<t
, we say that
s
is covered by
t
, or
t
covers
s
, and we write
s\lessdot t
. We denote by
\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{P})
the set of pairs
(s,t)\in\mathrm{P}\times\mathrm{P}
such that
s\lessdot t.
An edge labeling of
\mathrm{P}
is a map
\lambda:\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{P})\rightarrow\Lambda
, with
\Lambda
a totally ordered set with total order denoted
\vartriangleleft
. An edge labeling of
\mathrm{P}
allows us to associate to every saturated chain
C
of the form

s=u_{0}\lessdot u_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot u_{k}=t

between two elements
s\leqslant t
the list of labels
\lambda(C)\coloneqq\lambda(u_{0},u_{1})\ldots\lambda(u_{k-1},u_{k}).

Björner and Wachs introduced the notion of edge-lexicographical labeling on a finite graded bounded poset
\mathrm{P}
in
[
9
]
.

Definition 2.1

An edge labeling
\lambda:\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{P})\rightarrow\Lambda
of
\mathrm{P}
is called
edge-lexicographical
(EL), if for all
s\leqslant t
in
\mathrm{P}
, there exists a unique maximal chain
C

s=u_{0}\lessdot u_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot u_{k}=t

between
s
and
t
with weakly increasing labels as the rank increases:

\lambda(u_{0},u_{1})\trianglelefteqslant\lambda(u_{1},u_{2})\trianglelefteqslant\cdots\trianglelefteqslant\lambda(u_{k-2},u_{k-1})\trianglelefteqslant\lambda(u_{k-1},u_{k}),

and the maximal chain
C
is lexicographically minimal among all maximal chains from
s
to
t
.
3
3

3

For the definition of EL-labelings, different conventions exist in the literature: we can require the unique maximal chain to have either weakly or strictly increasing labels. One must then adapt the definition of descent (Subsection
2.3
) accordingly.

An
EL-labeled poset

(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is a pair consisting of a poset
\mathrm{P}
and an EL-labeling

\lambda:\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{P})\rightarrow\Lambda.

For all
s\leqslant t
in
\mathrm{P}
with EL-labeling
\lambda
, the interval
[s,t]
has a natural EL-labeling
\lambda_{s,t}
given by the restriction of
\lambda
to the covering relations in
[s,t]
.

It is well-known that the order complex
\Delta(\mathrm{P})
of an EL-labeled poset
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is shellable, with shelling order on the maximal faces given by the lexicographic order induced by
\lambda
on the maximal chains of
\mathrm{P}
. For general background on posets and topology, we refer to
[
55
]
.

2.2.
Face enumeration on simplicial complexes

Let
\Delta
be a finite simplicial complex of dimension
d-1
. For each integer
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,d\}
let us denote by
f_{i}(\Delta)
the number of simplices in
\Delta
of cardinality
i
(that is, of dimension
i-1
).
The
f
-vector of
\Delta
is the
(d+1)
-tuple
(f_{0}(\Delta),f_{1}(\Delta),\dots,f_{d}(\Delta))
. The
h
-vector of
\Delta
, denoted
(h_{0}(\Delta),\ldots,h_{d}(\Delta))
, is defined by the following equation

\sum_{i=0}^{d}f_{i}(\Delta)(y-1)^{d-i}\;=\;\sum_{i=0}^{d}h_{i}(\Delta)y^{d-i}.

The
f
-polynomial
f(\Delta;x)
and the
h
-polynomial
h(\Delta;y)
are defined as

f(\Delta;x)\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{d}f_{i}\>x^{i},\quad\text{ and }\quad h(\Delta;y)\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{d}h_{i}\>y^{i}.

By definition, the relation between the
f
-polynomial and the
h
-polynomial can be written as follows:

h(\Delta;y)\;=\;(1-y)^{d}\,f\left(\frac{y}{1-y}\right).

2.3.
Flag enumeration on posets

In this section, we recall the notion of flag
f
-vectors of posets. For general background on flag vectors and further information on the topics covered in this section, we refer to
[
52
, Chapter 3]
.

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a finite graded poset with rank function
\operatorname{rk}
, and assume that
\mathrm{P}
has rank
n
. For a subset
S=\{r_{1}<\cdots<r_{m}\}\subseteq\{0,\dots,n\}
, the

flag
f
-vector
of
\mathrm{P}
is the map
\alpha_{\mathrm{P}}:2^{\{0,\ldots,n\}}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
defined by:

\alpha_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\coloneqq\left\lvert\>\left\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{m}\in\mathrm{P}:\operatorname{rk}(s_{i})=r_{i}\text{ for }1\leqslant i\leqslant m\,\right\}\>\right\rvert.

The
flag
h
-vector
of
\mathrm{P}
is the map
\beta_{\mathrm{P}}\colon 2^{\{0,\dots,n\}}\to\mathbb{Z}
defined by any of the two equivalent conditions

\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\coloneqq\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S|-|T|}\,\alpha_{\mathrm{P}}(T),\qquad\text{or}\qquad\alpha_{\mathrm{P}}(S)=\sum_{T\subseteq S}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(T).

If
\mathrm{P}
has
\widehat{0}
and
\widehat{1}
, one has:

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{P}}(S)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mathrm{P}}(S\cap[n-1])

\displaystyle\text{for all $S$; and}

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{whenever $S\not\subseteq[n-1]$ (i.e., $0\in S$ or $n\in S$).}

If
\Delta=\Delta(\mathrm{P})
is the order complex of a finite graded poset
\mathrm{P}
, then the vectors
\alpha_{\mathrm{P}}
and
\beta_{\mathrm{P}}
can be used to enumerate the faces of
\Delta
according to dimension:

f_{i}(\Delta)=\sum_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert=i}\alpha_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\qquad\text{and}\qquad h_{i}(\Delta)=\sum_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert=i}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S).

Therefore, the
f
-polynomial and the
h
-polynomial of
\mathrm{P}
can be written as

(1)

f(\Delta;x)=\sum_{i}\left(\sum_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert=i}\alpha_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\right)x^{i}\quad\text{ and }\quad h(\Delta;y)=\sum_{i}\left(\sum_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert=i}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\right)y^{i}.

If the poset
\mathrm{P}
admits an EL-labeling
\lambda:\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{P})\rightarrow\Lambda,
by a result of Stanley appearing in
[
10
, Theorem 2.7]
it is possible to interpret the entries of the flag
h
-vector of
\mathrm{P}
in terms of statistics of
\lambda
, as we now explain. A sequence of labels

S=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda

has a
descent
at
i
for some
i\in[n-1]
, if
\lambda_{i}\vartriangleright\lambda_{i+1}
. The sequence has descent set
\mathrm{Des}(S)
and descent count
\mathrm{des}(S)
:

\mathrm{Des}(S)\coloneqq\{i\in[n-1]\mid S\textrm{ has a descent at }i\},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{des}(S)\coloneqq\left\lvert\mathrm{Des}(S)\right\rvert.

Lemma 2.2

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a finite graded bounded poset of rank
n
which admits an EL-labeling
\lambda
. For every subset
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, the flag
h
-vector entry
\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)
equals the number of maximal chains with descent set
S
with respect to
\lambda
.

The above result (proved in the more general context of R-labelings) can also be found in
[
52
, Theorem 3.14.2]
.

2.4.
Chow polynomials via flag enumeration

The Chow polynomial
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{M}}(x)
of a matroid
\mathrm{M}
was defined in
[
29
]
as the Hilbert–Poincaré series of an Artinian graded algebra
\underline{\textrm{CH}}(\mathrm{M})
, called the
Chow ring
of
\mathrm{M}
. The Chow ring of a matroid
\mathrm{M}
was introduced by Feichtner and Yuzvinksy
[
28
]
as the Chow ring of the smooth and generally noncomplete toric variety associated with the Bergman fan of
\mathrm{M}
. Similarly, the augmented Chow polynomial
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}(x)
was defined as the Hilbert–Poincaré series of another Artinian graded algebra
\mathrm{CH}(\mathrm{M})
, called the
augmented Chow ring
of
\mathrm{M}
. The augmented Chow ring of a matroid
\mathrm{M}
is introduced by
[
11
]
as the Chow ring of the smooth and generally noncomplete toric variety associated with the augmented Bergman fan of
\mathrm{M}
.

In
[
30
]
a more general definition of Chow polynomial for finite bounded graded posets is introduced. Precisely, the Chow polynomial of a poset
\mathrm{P}
is defined recursively by
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)=1
whenever
|\mathrm{P}|=1
, and

\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\mathrm{P}\setminus\{\widehat{0}\}\end{subarray}}\overline{\chi}_{\widehat{0},s}(x)\,\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{s,\widehat{1}}(x)

otherwise. Here,
\overline{\chi}_{st}(x)
stands for the
reduced
characteristic polynomial of an interval
[s,t]\subseteq\mathrm{P}
. The augmented Chow polynomial
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
is defined, correspondingly, as:

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{P}}x^{\operatorname{rk}(s)}\,\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{s,\widehat{1}}(x).

A non-obvious property of Chow polynomials and augmented Chow polynomials is that their coefficients always form a nonnegative unimodal palindromic sequence (see
[
30
, Theorem 1.3]
). In the special case of matroids, the nonnegativity is a consequence of the (augmented) Chow polynomial being a Hilbert–Poincaré series, whereas the symmetry and the unimodality follow from Poincaré duality and the hard Lefschetz theorem proved for (augmented) Chow rings, see
[
1
,
12
]
.

Thanks to the following result by Ferroni, Matherne, and Vecchi, the Chow and the augmented Chow polynomial of a poset can be read off of the flag
h
-vector of stable sets. A set
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
is
stable
if there exists no integer
i
such that
i\in S
and
i+1\in S
.
4
4

4

The etymology of “stable” is as follows: in a graph, a stable set of vertices is a collection of vertices no two of which are adjacent. In the Hasse diagram of the poset
\mathbb{Z}
with its natural order, the stable sets are precisely those subsets that contain no pair of consecutive integers.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
30
, Theorem 4.25]
)

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a finite graded bounded poset of rank
n
. Then,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\{2,\ldots,n-1\}\\
S\text{ stable}\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\,x^{|S|}\,(1+x)^{n-1-2|S|},

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\\
S\text{ stable}\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\,x^{|S|}\,(1+x)^{n-2|S|}.

The preceding result can be recast in terms of the so-called
\gamma
-polynomials. Recall that a polynomial
f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}a_{i}\,x^{i}
(where we allow
a_{d}=0
) is said to be
symmetric
(or
palindromic
) with center of symmetry
d/2
if
a_{i}=a_{d-i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
(here we are implicitly defining
a_{i}=0
if
i<0
or
i>d
). This symmetry condition can be expressed succinctly by the equation
f(x)=x^{d}\,f(x^{-1})
.

A linear basis for symmetric polynomials with center of symmetry
d/2
is given by the collection
\left\{x^{i}(1+x)^{d-2i}\right\}_{i=0}^{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}
. Therefore, if
f(x)
is symmetric with center of symmetry
d/2
, we can write

f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\gamma_{i}\,x^{i}(1+x)^{d-2i},

for some unique real numbers
\gamma_{0},\ldots,\gamma_{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}
. The
\gamma
-polynomial
associated to
f(x)
is the univariate polynomial

\gamma(f;y)\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\gamma_{i}\>y^{i}.

If the
\gamma
-polynomial of
f(x)
has nonnegative coefficients, we say that
f(x)
is
\gamma
-positive
. A routine check shows that if
f(x)
is
\gamma
-positive, then it is also unimodal. In particular, Theorem
2.3
implies that if the flag
h
-vector of a poset is nonnegative, then the Chow and the augmented Chow polynomials are
\gamma
-positive.

A very useful application of Theorem
2.3
is the following formula for Chow and augmented Chow polynomials of poset duals.

Lemma 2.4

(Duality Lemma)

For any finite graded bounded poset
\mathrm{P}
, the following formulas hold:

\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}^{*}}(x)=\mathrm{H}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathrm{P})}(x)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}^{*}}(x)=\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x).

Equivalently,

\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}^{*}};y)=\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathrm{P})};y)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}^{*}};y)=\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y).

Proof.

Let
n
be the rank of
\mathrm{P}
. In the non-augmented case,

\displaystyle\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}^{\ast}};y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\textrm{ stable}\\
1\notin S\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}^{\ast}}(S)y^{|S|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\textrm{ stable}\\
1\notin S\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(n-S)y^{|S|}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\textrm{ stable}\\
n-1\notin S\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)y^{|S|}=\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathrm{P})};y).

In the augmented case,

\displaystyle\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}^{\ast}};y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\textrm{ stable}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}^{\ast}}(S)y^{|S|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\textrm{ stable}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(n-S)y^{|S|}=\sum_{S\textrm{ stable}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)y^{|S|}=\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y).\qed

2.5.
Interlacing polynomials

Let
f
be a monic real-rooted polynomial of degree
n
and
g
a monic real-rooted polynomial of degree
n
or
n+1
, with ordered roots

\alpha_{n}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\alpha_{1},\quad(\beta_{n+1}\leqslant)\beta_{n}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\beta_{1}

respectively (ignoring
\beta_{n+1}
if
g
is of degree
n
). We say that
f

interlaces

g
, denoted by
f\preceq g
, if

(\beta_{n+1}\leqslant\>)\>\alpha_{n}\leqslant\beta_{n}\leqslant\alpha_{n-1}\leqslant\beta_{n-1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\alpha_{1}\leqslant\beta_{1}.

We say that the
zeros of
f
and
g
interlace
if either
f\preceq g
, or
g\preceq f
. By convention, the zeros of any two polynomials of degree
0
or
1
interlace,
0\preceq f,f\preceq 0
and
0\preceq 0
for all real-rooted polynomials
f
of arbitrary degree.

A classical analytic criterion of interlacing polynomials is the following positivity condition: If
g\preceq f
, then the Wronskian of
f
and
g
is nonnegative over all real numbers

\textrm{W}[f,g]\coloneqq\det\begin{pmatrix}f^{\prime}&g^{\prime}\\
f&g\end{pmatrix}\geqslant 0.

The converse is true if the multiplicity of every root of
f
and
g
is
1
.

The main motivation behind the notion of interlacing polynomials is the following result, often called Obreschkoff’s theorem, which allows us to produce new real-rooted polynomials using interlacing sequences of polynomials.

Theorem 2.5

(
[
42
, Satz 5.2]
)

Let
f,g
be nonzero real-rooted polynomials. The zeros of
f
and
g
interlace if and only if any polynomial in the linear space spanned by
f,g

\left\{\>\alpha\>f+\beta\>g\mid\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}\>\right\}

only has real roots.

This result will be instrumental in the sequel. The following properties of interlacing polynomials are due to Wagner.

Proposition 2.6

(
[
56
]
)

Let
f,g,h
be real-rooted polynomials in
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}[x]
. The following properties hold:

(1)

(Convexity) If
f\preceq g,h
, then for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
,
f\preceq\alpha g+\beta h
.

(2)

(Convexity) If
g,h\preceq f
, then for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
,
\alpha g+\beta h\preceq f
.

(3)

(Adding zero) If
f\preceq g
, then
g\preceq xf
.

(4)

(Interpolation) If
f\preceq g
, then for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
,
f\preceq\alpha f+\beta g\preceq g
.

Definition 2.7

A family of real-rooted polynomials
F_{n}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
with nonnegative coefficients is an
interlacing family
or
interlacing sequence
if and only if
f_{i}\preceq f_{j}
for all
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
.

Our main tool to produce new interlacing sequences out of known interlacing sequences is the following more general theorem about linear operators preserving sequences of interlacing polynomials with nonnegative coefficients.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
18
, Theorem 7.8.5]
)

Let
\mathbf{G}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}G_{ij}(x)\end{pmatrix}
be an
m\times n
matrix of real polynomials
G_{ij}(x)
. Then multiplication by
\mathbf{G}(x)
preserves interlacing sequences of polynomials with nonnegative coefficients if and only if

(1)

each polynomial
G_{ij}(x)
has positive coefficients for all
i\in[m]
and all
j\in[n]
, and

(2)

for all
1\leqslant i_{1}<i_{2}\leqslant m
and all
1\leqslant j_{1}<j_{2}\leqslant n
we have the interlacing relation

(px+q)G_{i_{1}j_{2}}(x)+G_{i_{2}j_{2}}(x)\preceq(px+q)G_{i_{1}j_{1}}(x)+G_{i_{2}j_{1}}(x)

for all real numbers
p,q>0.

We provide a short proof of the following lemma, a slight generalization of
[
18
, Corollary 7.8.7]
, which shows that certain matrices of specific shapes satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
2.8
. Those matrices will appear in the proofs of Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
5.1
.

Lemma 2.9

Let
m
and
n
be positive integers, and let
d_{1}\leqslant d_{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant d_{m}
be a weakly increasing sequence of integers.
Let
\mathbf{A}(x)
be an
m\times n
matrix with polynomial entries defined as

\mathbf{A}(x)\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}a_{ij}(x)\end{pmatrix},\text{ where }a_{ij}(x)=\begin{cases}\alpha^{i}\cdot x&\text{ if }j\leqslant d_{i},\\
\alpha^{i}&\text{ if }j>d_{i},\end{cases}\quad\alpha^{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}.

For any fixed column number
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
, any two rows
1\leqslant i<i^{\prime}\leqslant m
, and nonnegative real numbers
p,q
, we write
f_{j}(x)
for the polynomial

\displaystyle f_{j}(\mathbf{A};p,q,i,i^{\prime};x)\coloneqq(p\>x+q)\>a_{i\>j}(x)+a_{i^{\prime}\>j}(x).

Then for any two column numbers
1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n
, and any nonnegative real numbers
p,q\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, we have that

f_{j^{\prime}}\preceq f_{j}.

Proof.

By monotonicity of the sequence
(d_{k})_{k=1}^{m}
, we have
d_{i}\leqslant d_{i^{\prime}}
.
By definition of the matrix
A
, there are
5
cases, depending on the relative positions of
j<j^{\prime}
with respect to
d_{i}\leqslant d_{i^{\prime}}
. For each of the cases, we fix
\alpha^{i},\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, and consider the
2\times 2
submatrix

\mathbf{A}_{i,\>i^{\prime},\>j,\>j^{\prime}}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}a_{i\>j}&a_{i\>j^{\prime}}\\
a_{i^{\prime}\>j}&a_{i^{\prime}\>j^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}.

(1)

If
j<j^{\prime}\leqslant d_{i}\leqslant d_{i^{\prime}}
, then we have

\mathbf{A}_{i,\>i^{\prime},\>j,\>j^{\prime}}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{i}\>x&\alpha^{i}\>x\\
\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\>x&\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\>x\end{pmatrix}.

This implies that
f_{j}=f_{j^{\prime}}
for all
p,q\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(2)

If
j\leqslant d_{i}<j^{\prime}\leqslant d_{i^{\prime}}
, then we have

\mathbf{A}_{i,\>i^{\prime},\>j,\>j^{\prime}}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{i}\>x&\alpha^{i}\\
\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\>x&\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\>x\end{pmatrix}.

This implies that

\displaystyle f_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=(px+q)\alpha^{i}x+\alpha^{i^{\prime}}x,\text{ with roots }-\frac{\alpha^{i}q+\alpha^{i^{\prime}}}{\alpha^{i}p},\text{ and }0,

\displaystyle f_{j^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle=(px+q)\alpha^{i}+\alpha^{i^{\prime}}x,\text{ with root }-\frac{\alpha^{i}q}{\alpha^{i}p+\alpha^{i^{\prime}}}.

This implies that
f_{j^{\prime}}\preceq f_{j}
for all
p,q\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(3)

If
d_{i}<j<j^{\prime}\leqslant d_{i^{\prime}}
, then we have

\mathbf{A}_{i,\>i^{\prime},\>j,\>j^{\prime}}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{i}&\alpha^{i}\\
\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\>x&\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\>x\end{pmatrix}.

This implies that
f_{j^{\prime}}(x)=f_{j}(x)
for all
p,q\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(4)

If
d_{i}<j\leqslant d_{i^{\prime}}<j^{\prime}
, then we have

\mathbf{A}_{i,\>i^{\prime},\>j,\>j^{\prime}}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{i}&\alpha^{i}\\
\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\>x&\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}.

This implies that

\displaystyle f_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=(px+q)\alpha^{i}+\alpha^{i^{\prime}}x,\text{ with root}-\frac{\alpha^{i}q}{\alpha^{i}p+\alpha^{i^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle f_{j^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle=(px+q)\alpha^{i}+\alpha^{i^{\prime}},\text{ with root}-\frac{\alpha^{i}q+\alpha^{i^{\prime}}}{\alpha^{i}p}.

This implies that
f_{j^{\prime}}\preceq f_{j}
for all
p,q\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(5)

If
d_{i}\leqslant d_{i^{\prime}}<j<j^{\prime}
, then we have

\mathbf{A}_{i,\>i^{\prime},\>j,\>j^{\prime}}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{i}&\alpha^{i}\\
\alpha^{i^{\prime}}&\alpha^{i^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}.

This implies that
f_{j^{\prime}}=f_{j}
for all
p,q\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. ∎

We also have the following useful corollary of Theorem
2.8
.

Corollary 2.10

(
[
18
, Corollary 7.8.6]
)

Let
A
be an
m\times n
matrix with nonnegative real entries. Then
A
preserves interlacing families of polynomials with nonnegative coefficients if and only if all two-by-two minors of
A
are nonnegative.

Finally, we have the following two results stating that the
\gamma
-transformation preserves real-rootedness and interlacing polynomials.

Lemma 2.11

(
[
17
]
,
[
33
]
)

If
f(x)
is a symmetric polynomial then the following are equivalent:

(1)

All the roots of
f(x)
are real and nonpositive.

(2)

All the roots of
\gamma(f;y)
are real and nonpositive.

Lemma 2.12

(
[
36
, Proposition 2.5]
)

If
f(x),g(x)
are palindromic polynomials with degree
\deg(g)=\deg(f)+1
, then the following are equivalent:

(1)

f(x)\preceq g(x)
.

(2)

\gamma(f;y)\preceq\gamma(g;y)
.

Using these two lemmas above, we will prove our main theorem about real-rootedness of Chow polynomials by proving the corresponding statements for their
\gamma
-polynomials in
Section

5
. This will be done by expressing those polynomials as nonnegative sums of certain sequences of polynomials, and then proving that those sequences are interlacing by proving that they satisfy a linear recursion satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem
2.8
.

3.
Rank-uniform labeled posets

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a bounded finite graded poset with rank function
\operatorname{rk}\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. For every element
s
and for every nonnegative integer
k
, we write
W_{k}(s)
for the number of elements of rank
k+\operatorname{rk}s
above
s
, called the
k
-th Whitney number above
s
. The poset
\mathrm{P}
is
rank-uniform
if for all
s,t\in\mathrm{P}
of the same rank and for every positive integer
k
, the number of elements of rank
k+\operatorname{rk}s
above
s
is equal to the number of elements of rank
k+\operatorname{rk}s
above
t
:

W_{k}(s)=W_{k}(t).

In this section we will introduce an edge-labeled version of the above definition, which applies to a poset equipped with an EL-labeling. We start with some terminology related to EL-labelings.

Definition 3.1

(Width, index, descent)

Let
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
be an EL-labeled poset and let
s\in\mathrm{P}
. Denote by
\lambda_{1}\vartriangleleft\cdots\vartriangleleft\lambda_{\ell}
the labels of the covering relations above
s
.

•

The
width above
s
is the function

\omega_{s}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0},\quad\omega_{s}(i)\coloneqq\left\lvert\>\{t\in\mathrm{P}\mid s\lessdot t\>,\>\lambda(s,t)=\lambda_{i}\>\}\>\right\rvert.

In other words, the width above
s
at index
i
records the number of covers of
s
labeled by
\lambda_{i}
(see Figure
1
below). By convention, for
i>\ell
,
\omega_{s}(i)=0
.

s
\lambda_{1}
\lambda_{1}
\lambda_{2}
\lambda_{2}
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\omega_{s}(1)
\omega_{s}(2)

Figure 1.
Widths above an element
s
.

•

Let
t
cover
s
in
\mathrm{P}
. We denote the
index
of the label
\lambda(s,t)
in
[\ell]
by

\mathrm{Ind}(s,t)\coloneqq\text{ the unique index $i\in[\ell]$ such that }\lambda_{i}=\lambda(s,t).

•

Let
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\vartriangleleft\cdots\vartriangleleft\lambda_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\prime}
be the labels of the covering relations above
t
. We denote by
\mathrm{Des}_{s}(t)
the set of indices
j
in
[\ell^{\prime}]
such that
\lambda_{j}\vartriangleleft\lambda(s,t)
, that is, which gives rise to a descent, and we write
\mathrm{des}_{s}(t)
for the cardinality of that set.

\mathrm{Des}_{s}(t)\coloneqq\{\>j\in[\ell^{\prime}]\mid\lambda_{j}^{\prime}\vartriangleleft\lambda(s,t)\},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{des}_{s}(t)\coloneqq\left\lvert\>\mathrm{Des}(s,t)\>\right\rvert.

The central object of study in this article is the following class of posets.

Definition 3.2

(Rank-uniform labeled poset)

Let
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
be an EL-labeled poset. We say that
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is
rank-uniform
if the following holds.

•

(Uniform width) For all
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{P}
of same rank and for all
i\geqslant 1
, the widths are equal:
\omega_{s}(i)=\omega_{s^{\prime}}(i).

•

(Uniform descent) For all
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{P}
of same rank and for every covering relations
s\lessdot t
and
s^{\prime}\lessdot t^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{Ind}(s,t)=\mathrm{Ind}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
, the descent counts are equal:
\mathrm{des}_{s}(t)=\mathrm{des}_{s^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}).

If
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is rank-uniform, for any
0\leqslant k\leqslant\operatorname{rk}(\mathrm{P})
and any
i\geqslant 1
we denote
\omega_{k}(i)\coloneqq\omega_{s}(i)
for any
s\in\mathrm{P}
of rank
k
. Similarly we denote
\mathrm{des}_{k}(i)\coloneqq\mathrm{des}_{s}(t)
for any
s
of rank
k
and any
t\gtrdot s
such that
\mathrm{Ind}(s,t)=i
. In other words,
\mathrm{des}_{k}(i)
records the number of indices of descents immediately above rank
k
in
\mathrm{P}
which starts with the
i
-th label among all labels at rank
k
.

Example 3.3

Consider the labeled poset
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
whose labeled Hasse diagram is drawn in Figure
2
below.

1
2
1
2
1
2
1
1
1

Figure 2.
A rank-uniform labeled poset.

One can check that
\lambda
is an EL-labeling. For all element
s\in\mathrm{P}
of rank
0
or
1
we have
\omega_{s}(1)=1
,
\omega_{s}(2)=1
and
\omega_{s}(i)=0
for all
i>2.
Moreover, for all element
s\in\mathrm{P}
of rank
2
we have
\omega_{s}(1)=1
and
\omega_{s}(i)=0
for all
i>1
(which is more generally true for any
s
of corank
1
in a labeled poset). This proves that
\mathrm{P}
has uniform widths.

For all element
s\in\mathrm{P}
of rank
0
or
1
we have
\mathrm{des}_{s}(1)=0
,
\mathrm{des}_{s}(2)=1
and
\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)=0
for all
i\geqslant 2.
Moreover, for all element
s\in\mathrm{P}
of rank
2
we have
\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)=0
for all
i\geqslant 1
(which is more generally true for any
s
of corank
1
in a labeled poset). This proves that
\mathrm{P}
has uniform descents as well, and so
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is a rank-uniform labeled poset.

Example 3.4

(Uniform matroids)

Let
n\geqslant k\geqslant 1
be integers, and consider the poset given by all the subsets of
[n]
of cardinality
\leqslant k-1
or cardinality
n
, ordered by inclusion. This is the lattice of flats of the rank
k
uniform matroid of size
n
, denoted
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{U}_{k,n})
. This poset has an EL-labeling
\lambda:\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{U}_{k,n}))\rightarrow[n]
defined by

\lambda(S\lessdot T)\coloneqq\min T\setminus S.

Let us check that
(\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{U}_{k,n}),\lambda)
is a rank-uniform labeled poset. For all element
s\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{U}_{k,n})
of rank
p\leqslant k-2
and all
i\geqslant 1
, we have
\omega_{s}(i)=1
if
i\leqslant n-k
and
\omega_{s}(i)=0
otherwise. This proves uniformity of widths.

For every element
s\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{U}_{k,n})
of rank
p\leqslant k-3
and all
i\geqslant 1,
we have
\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)=i-1
if
i\leqslant n-k
and
\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)=0
otherwise. This proves uniformity of descents, since the uniformity of descents at rank
\geqslant k-2
is trivially verified.

In
[
23
,
24
]
, Dowling introduced a generalization of partition lattices called Dowling geometries. We highlight this class and its EL-labeling in the example below. Dowling geometries are examples of rank-uniform supersolvable lattices, which we discuss in detail in
Section

6
.

Example 3.5

(Dowling geometries)

Let
n\geqslant 1
be a positive integer, and let
G
be a finite group. A
G
-prepartition
of
[n]
is a pair
(\mu,\varphi)
consisting of a partition

0\sqcup[n]=\mu_{0}\sqcup\mu_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mu_{k},

with
0\in\mu_{0}
, and a labeling function

\varphi\colon\mu_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mu_{k}\rightarrow\textrm{G}.

The unique block
\mu_{0}
containing
0
is called the zero block and the others the non-zero blocks.
G
-prepartitions are ordered by setting
(\mu,\varphi)\geqslant(\mu^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
if every non-zero block of
\mu
is a union of non-zero blocks of
\mu^{\prime}
and the labelings coincide. Two
G
-prepartitions
(\mu,\varphi)
and
(\mu^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
are said to be equivalent if
\mu=\mu^{\prime}
and for each block
\mu_{i}=\mu^{\prime}_{i}
there exists an element
g\in\textrm{G}
such that we have the equality
\varphi_{|\mu_{i}}=g\cdot\varphi^{\prime}_{|\mu^{\prime}_{i}}
. A
G
-partition is an equivalence class of
G
-partitions, and the Dowling geometry
\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})
is the set of
G
-partitions of
[n]
with the order induced by
\leqslant
. For instance, if
G
is the trivial group then a
G
-partition is simply a partition of
0\sqcup[n]
, and
\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})
is the
(n+1)
-th partition lattice
\Pi_{n+1}.
More generally, if
G
is a finite cyclic group then
\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})
is the intersection lattice of the arrangement consisting of the hyperplanes defined by equations of the form

\displaystyle\{\>x_{i}=g\>x_{j}\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n,g\in G\>\}\cup\{\>x_{k}=0\mid 1\leqslant k\leqslant n\>\}.

For instance, if
\textrm{G}=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, we get the
n
-th type B braid arrangement.

Consider the poset
\Pi^{\textrm{B}}_{3}
whose Hasse diagram is drawn in Figure
3
. This is the intersection lattice of the type B braid arrangement of rank
3
, defined by the hyperplanes

\bigg\{H_{ij}^{\pm}\coloneqq V(x_{i}\pm x_{j})\mid 1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 3\bigg\}\cup\bigg\{H_{k}\coloneqq V(x_{k})\mid 1\leqslant k\leqslant 3\bigg\}.

More concretely, the poset
\Pi^{\textrm{B}}_{3}
consists of all the intersections of these hyperplanes, ordered by reverse inclusion, and ranked by codimension:

(1)

In rank
0
, the only intersection space is the full space
\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

(2)

In rank
1
, the intersection spaces are the
9
hyperplanes of the form
H_{i}
and
H_{jk}^{\pm}
.

(3)

In rank
2
, the intersection spaces are
13
lines:

•

the
3
coordinate lines:
H_{i}\cap H_{j}
.

•

the
6
lines that are the intersections of one coordinate hyperplane with one of the diagonals:
H_{k}\cap H_{ij}^{\pm}
,

•

the
4
lines that are the intersections of pairs of diagonals
H_{ij}^{\pm}\cap H_{jk}^{\pm}
.

(4)

In rank
3
, the only intersection space is the origin.

These intersections are encoded using signed partitions as follows: the zero block contains indices
i
with
x_{i}=0
and the nonzero block contains indices whose coordinates are equal up to a common sign shift. For example, the block
03
represents the hyperplane given by
x_{3}=0
, and the block
1\overline{2}
represents the hyperplane given by
x_{1}=-x_{2}
. Therefore,
03\mid 1\overline{2}
corresponds to the intersection space
H_{3}\cap H_{12}^{-}
.

.

In
[
48
]
, Rodica Simion introduced an EL-labeling on Dowling geometries, which endows Dowling lattices with the structure of rank-uniform labeled posets. Let
\overline{(\mu,\varphi_{\mu})}\lessdot\overline{(\mu^{\prime},\varphi_{\mu^{\prime}}})
be a covering relation in
\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})
, which is a merge of two
G
-blocks in
(\mu,\varphi_{\mu})
. Then

\lambda\left(\overline{(\mu,\varphi_{\mu})},\overline{(\mu^{\prime},\varphi_{\mu^{\prime}})}\right)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\min\mu_{j}&\text{if $\overline{(\mu_{i},\varphi_{i})},\overline{(\mu_{j},\varphi_{j})}$ are merging, with $\min\mu_{i}<\min\mu_{j}$}\\
\min\mu_{i}&\text{if $\overline{(\mu_{i},\varphi_{i})},\mu_{0}$ are merging.}\end{cases}

We compute the widths and descents using this labeling for
\Pi_{2}^{\textrm{B}}
; see
Figure

4
. For the unique element
\widehat{0}
of rank
0
,
\omega_{0}(1)=\omega_{\widehat{0}}(1)=1
,
\omega_{0}(2)=\omega_{\widehat{0}}(2)=3
, and
\mathrm{des}_{0}(1)=0
,
\mathrm{des}_{0}(2)=1
. For any
s
of rank
1
,
\omega_{1}=\omega_{s}(1)=1
.

.

We have the following lemma relating this labeled notion of uniformity to the classical, non-labeled notion of rank-uniformity for posets.

Lemma 3.6

If an EL-labeled poset
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is rank-uniform, then
\mathrm{P}
is rank-uniform.

Proof.

We prove that
W_{k}(s)
only depends on the rank of
s
and
k
. Since
\lambda
is an EL-labeling of
\mathrm{P}
, every element
t
of rank
k
above
s
corresponds to a unique maximal chain from
s
to
t
of length
k

s=s_{0}\lessdot s_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{k}=t

with weakly increasing labels
\lambda(s_{0},s_{1})\trianglelefteqslant\cdots\trianglelefteqslant\lambda(s_{k-1},s_{k})
.
Therefore,

W_{k}(s)=\left\lvert\{s=s_{0}\lessdot s_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{k}\,|\,\lambda(s_{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}}},s_{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}}+1})\textrm{ weakly increasing}\}\>\right\rvert.

One can refine the quantity on the right by the index of the last label
\lambda(s_{k-1},s_{k})
in the total order
\vartriangleleft
as follows: For every
i
, we write

W_{k}^{i}(s)\coloneqq\left\lvert\>\{s=s_{0}\lessdot s_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{k}\,|\,\lambda(s_{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}}},s_{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}}+1})\textrm{ weakly increasing},\mathrm{Ind}(s_{k-1},s_{k})=i\}\>\right\rvert,

and prove by induction on
k
that
W_{k}^{i}(s)=W_{k}^{i}(s^{\prime})
as soon as
\operatorname{rk}s=\operatorname{rk}s^{\prime}
.

The base case is when
k=\operatorname{rk}(s)+1
. The number of covers of
s
whose covering relation is labeled by the
i
-th label is the width number
w_{s}(i)
which only depends on
\operatorname{rk}s
and
i
by definition of a rank-uniform labeled poset. Let
k\geqslant\operatorname{rk}(s)+1
and assume that
W_{j}^{i}(s)
on depends on
\operatorname{rk}s
for all
j\leqslant k
and all
i
. Then, we can refine the set

\{s=s_{0}\lessdot s_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{k+1}\,|\,\lambda(s_{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}}},s_{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}}+1})\textrm{ weakly increasing},\mathrm{Ind}(s_{k},s_{k+1})=i\}

by the index of the penultimate label
\mathrm{Ind}(s_{k-1},s_{k})
. This gives the following recursive formula

W_{k+1}^{i}(s)=\sum_{j\geqslant 1}\>W_{k}^{j}(s)\>\cdot\left(\sum_{i\geqslant\mathrm{des}_{k}(j)}\omega_{k}(i)\right).

Since
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
has uniform widths and uniform descent counts, this shows that
W_{k+1}^{i}(s)=W_{k+1}^{i}(s^{\prime})
if
\operatorname{rk}s=\operatorname{rk}s^{\prime}
, which concludes the inductive step.
∎

An additional condition on a labeled poset
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
that will play a crucial role in this article is the
monotonicity
of the descent counts at a given rank, as a function of the label indices.

Definition 3.7

(Monotonic labeled poset)

Let
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
be a rank-uniform labeled poset, and for all
0\leqslant k\leqslant\operatorname{rk}(\mathrm{P})-1
denote by
\ell_{k}
the number of distinct labels above any element of rank
k
. The labeled poset
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is
monotonic
if for all
0\leqslant k\leqslant\operatorname{rk}(\mathrm{P})-1
the function
\mathrm{des}_{k}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}})
is weakly increasing on
[\ell_{k}]
. A poset
\mathrm{P}
admitting an EL-labeling
\lambda
such that
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is a monotonic rank-uniform labeled poset is called
uniformly monotonically edge-lexicographically shellable
, or
UMEL-shellable
for short.

In other words, a rank-uniform labeled poset
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is monotonic if at any element
s
in
\mathrm{P}
, the larger the label above
s
, the more descents we observe from this label.

Example 3.8

The rank-uniform labeled posets considered in Example
3.3
, Example
3.4
and Example
3.5
can be verified to be monotonic via routine checks.

In the next two sections, we will show that the
h
-polynomial and the
\gamma
-polynomial of a UMEL-shellable poset are real-rooted. We will do so by refining the
h
-polynomial and
\gamma
-polynomials as a positive sum of polynomials using face and flag enumeration. These refinement polynomials will satisfy a linear recursion with transition matrix as in Lemma
2.9
. In particular, the monotonicity condition on the EL-labeling ensures the Wronskian nonnegativity condition, which is exactly the condition guaranteeing that the transition matrix preserves interlacing. Let
\mathbf{A}(x)
be the transition matrix between two families of polynomials
\mathbf{g}_{m}(x)
and
\mathbf{h}_{n}(x)

\mathbf{h}_{n}(x)=\mathbf{A}(x)\cdot\mathbf{g}_{m}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}a_{ij}(x)\end{pmatrix}\cdot\mathbf{g}_{m}(x)

such that
\mathbf{A}
is an
n
-by-
m
matrix with nonnegative polynomial entries. By
2.8
, the requirement that two columns
j<j^{\prime}
satisfy, for all
p,q\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,

f_{j^{\prime}}\preceq f_{j}

is equivalent to the nonnegativity of the corresponding Wronskian over the real numbers

\textrm{W}[f_{j},f_{j^{\prime}}]=\det\begin{pmatrix}f_{j}^{\prime}&f_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}\\
f_{j}&f_{j^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}\geqslant 0.

The monotonicity condition on the
\mathrm{des}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}})
ensures the nonnegativity of values of Wronskians at all real numbers for all column pairs. Consequently,
\mathbf{A}(x)
preserves interlacing sequences, and the refinement polynomials form an interlacing sequence by induction, Lemma
2.9
, and Theorem
2.8
.

4.
Chain polynomials and
h
-polynomials are real-rooted

In this section, we prove that the chain polynomial and the
h
-polynomial of the order complex of a UMEL-shellable poset has only real and nonpositive zeros. This result unifies and extends several previously known instances of real-rootedness, including those arising from Dowling geometries, projective geometries, and uniform matroids. The definition of the interlacing family introduced here also prepare the ground for the recursive framework that we will use in
Section

5
.

Theorem
1.5

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a UMEL-shellable poset. The
h
-polynomial of the order complex
\Delta(\mathrm{P})
of
\mathrm{P}
only has real and nonpositive roots.

Proof.

Let
\lambda
be an EL-labeling of
\mathrm{P}
such that
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is a monotonic rank-uniform labeled poset. Let
s
be a nonmaximal element in
\mathrm{P}
and let
\lambda_{1}\vartriangleleft\cdots\vartriangleleft\lambda_{\ell}
denote the labels immediately above
s
. We write
\mathcal{C}_{s}
for the maximal chains of the interval
[s,\widehat{1}]
:

\mathcal{C}_{s}\coloneqq\{s=s_{0}\lessdot s_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{k}=\widehat{1}\}

We refine this set of maximal chains by the index of the first covering relation. For any
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
, we write
\mathcal{C}_{s}^{i}
for the maximal chains of the interval
[s,\widehat{1}]
such that the first covering relation is labeled by
\lambda_{i}
:

\mathcal{C}_{s}(i)\coloneqq\{\>s=s_{0}\lessdot s_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{k}=\widehat{1}_{s}\mid\lambda(s_{0},s_{1})=\lambda_{i}\}.

Let us denote

h_{s}(y)\coloneqq\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{s}}y^{\mathrm{des}(\lambda(C))}=h(\Delta([s,\widehat{1}])),

where the second equality comes from Lemma
2.2
and equation (
1
). We use the above refinement of
\mathcal{C}_{s}
to refine this polynomial. For any
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
, we write
h_{s}^{i}(y)
for the generating polynomial of maximal chains with first label index
i
in
[s,\widehat{1}]
with the descent statistics:

h_{s}^{i}(y)\coloneqq\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{s}(i)}y^{\mathrm{des}(\lambda(C))}.

By rank-uniformity of
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
, the vector of polynomials

\mathbf{h}_{s}(y)\coloneqq(h_{s}^{1}(y)\>,\>\ldots\>,\>h_{s}^{\ell}(y))

only depends on the corank
k
of
s
, and so we write

\mathbf{h}_{k}(y)=(h^{1}_{k}(y)\>,\>\ldots\>,\>h^{\ell}_{k}(y))

instead. If
n
is the rank of
\mathrm{P}
, we have

h(\Delta(\mathrm{P}),y)=\sum_{i\geqslant 1}^{\ell}h^{i}_{n}(y).

We will prove that
\mathbf{h}_{k}(y)
is interlacing for all
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
, by induction on the corank
k
. The result will then follow from Theorem
2.5
.

For the base case, we have that
\mathbf{h}_{1}(y)=(1)
, which is an interlacing family. For the inductive hypothesis, we assume that
\mathbf{h}_{k}(y)
is an interlacing family for some
k<n
, and let
s
be any element of
\mathrm{P}
of corank
k+1
. For every
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
, the number of covers of
s
with labels
\lambda_{i}
is the width of
s
at index
i

\omega_{s}(i)=\text{the number of $t$ in $[s,\widehat{1}]$ with $s\lessdot t$ such that $\lambda(s,t)=\lambda_{i}$}.

For each
i
, recall that
\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)
records the number of indices in the labels above covers of
s
labeled by
\lambda_{i}
that would give rise to a descent. Therefore, a cover of
s
labeled by
\lambda_{i}
followed by
\lambda_{j}^{\prime}
with
j\leqslant\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)
gives rise to a descent
\lambda_{i}\vartriangleright\lambda_{j}^{\prime}
, and a cover of
s
labeled by
\lambda_{i}
followed by
\lambda_{j}^{\prime}
with
j>\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)
gives rise to an ascent
\lambda_{i}\trianglelefteq\lambda_{j}^{\prime}
. Therefore, we have the following recursive formula

h_{s}^{i}(y)=\omega_{s}(i)\left(\sum_{j\leqslant\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)}\>y\>\cdot\>h_{k}^{j}(y)+\sum_{j>\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)}h_{k}^{j}(y)\right).

By the uniform-width condition on
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
, the statistics
\omega_{s}(i)
only depends on the corank of
s
. Therefore,

h_{k+1}^{i}(y)=\omega_{k+1}(i)\left(\sum_{j\leqslant\mathrm{des}_{k+1}(i)}\>y\>\cdot\>h_{k}^{j}(y)+\sum_{j>\mathrm{des}_{k+1}(i)}h_{k}^{j}(y)\right).

We can write this recursion in terms of a matrix-vector multiplication as follows:

\mathbf{h}_{k+1}(y)=\mathbf{A}_{k+1}(y)\cdot\mathbf{h}_{k}(y),

where the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{k+1}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{ij}\end{pmatrix}
is a matrix with polynomial entries defined by

a_{ij}(y)=\begin{cases}\omega_{k+1}(i)\cdot y&\text{ if }j\leqslant\mathrm{des}_{k+1}(i),\\
\omega_{k+1}(i)&\text{ if }j>\mathrm{des}_{k+1}(i).\end{cases}

By monotonicity of
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
the numbers
\mathrm{des}_{k+1}(i)
are weakly increasing in
i
, and so by
Lemma

2.9
, and Theorem
2.8
, the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{k+1}(y)
preserves the interlacing property. This shows that
\mathbf{h}_{k+1}(y)
is an interlacing sequence, which concludes the proof.
∎

5.
Chow polynomials and
\gamma
-polynomials are real-rooted

In this section, we prove that the Chow and augmented Chow polynomial of a UMEL-shellable poset only have real and nonpositive roots.

Theorem
1.2

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a UMEL-shellable poset.
Then the following statements hold:

(1)

The Chow polynomial
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
only has real and nonpositive roots.

(2)

The augmented Chow polynomial
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
only has real and nonpositive roots.

We prove the theorem by proving the following equivalent theorem for the respective
\gamma
-polynomials.

Theorem 5.1

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a UMEL-shellable poset.
The following statements hold.

(1)

The
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};y)
only has real and nonpositive roots.

(2)

The
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y)
only has real and nonpositive roots.

The upshot of passing to the
\gamma
-polynomials is that those polynomials count certain maximal chains of
\mathrm{P}
with the descent statistics, as explained in Sections
2.3
and
2.4
. We will prove
5.1
by providing a decomposition of the
\gamma
-polynomials into interlacing families of polynomials, given by refinements coming from the face and flag enumerations of the EL-labeled poset
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
, similarly to what we did in the previous section. These refinement polynomials will satisfy explicit linear recursive formulas, and we will use Theorem
2.8
to prove that those polynomials form interlacing sequences throughout the recursion.

5.1.
Refinements

Let
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
be a monotonic rank-uniform labeled poset of rank
n
. We introduce several sequences of interlacing polynomials associated to
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
. They are defined by counting the maximal chains of
\mathrm{P}
whose EL-label sequence have no double descents as follows.

Let
\mathcal{C}\coloneq\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P})
denote the collection of maximal chains of
\mathrm{P}
. If
S
is a subset of
[n-1]
, let
\mathcal{C}(S)
denote the collection of maximal chains of
\mathrm{P}
labeled by
\lambda
with descent set
S
:

\mathcal{C}(S)\coloneqq\{C\in\mathcal{C}\mid\mathrm{Des}(C)=S\}.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\textrm{ndd}}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{\textrm{ndd}}(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
denote the collection of maximal chains in
\mathrm{P}
labeled by
\lambda
with no double descent:

\mathcal{C}^{\textrm{ndd}}\coloneq\{\>C\in\mathcal{C}\mid\mathrm{Des}(C)\text{ is stable }\}.

By construction,

\mathcal{C}^{\textrm{ndd}}=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[n-1]\\
S\text{ stable}\end{subarray}}\>\mathcal{C}(S).

5.1.1.

Proposition 5.2

Let
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
be an EL-labeled poset of rank
n
. Then the
\gamma
-polynomial of
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
is the sum of all
\gamma^{n}_{i,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

The
\gamma
-polynomial of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
is the sum of all
\gamma^{n}_{i}(y)

\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma^{n}_{i}(y).

Proof.

By
2.3
, the
\gamma
-polynomial of the Chow polynomial of
\mathrm{P}
is given by

\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};\>y)\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
1\notin S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\>y^{|S|}.

By
Lemma

2.2
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
1\notin S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\>y^{|S|}\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
1\notin S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\mathcal{C}(S)\right\rvert\cdot y^{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}.

Grouping the maximal chains according to the index of their first label yields

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
1\notin S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\>y^{|S|}\;=\;\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
1\notin S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i}(S)\right\rvert\cdot y^{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}.

By the construction of the refining polynomials, we have

\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};\>y)\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
1\notin S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\>y^{|S|}\;=\;\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma^{n}_{i,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{4.97986pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}(y).

Note that the condition that
S
does not contain
1
implies that the edge labeling of the maximal chains begins with an ascent.

By
2.3
, the
\gamma
-polynomial of the augmented Chow polynomial is given by

\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y)\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\>y^{|S|}.

By
Lemma

2.2
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\textrm{ stable}\\
S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\>y^{|S|}\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\mathcal{C}(S)\right\rvert\cdot y^{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}.

Grouping the maximal chains according to the index of their first edge label gives

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\>y^{|S|}\;=\;\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i}(S)\right\rvert\cdot y^{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}.

By the construction of the refining polynomials, we have

\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y)\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\text{ stable}\\
S\subseteq[n-1]\end{subarray}}\beta_{\mathrm{P}}(S)\>y^{|S|}\;=\;\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\gamma^{n}_{i}(y).

Note that in this case, there is no restriction on whether the edge labeling of a maximal chain begins with an ascent or a descent.
∎

, we derive a recursive formula for the family
\boldsymbol{\bm{\gamma}}^{n}_{{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{4.97986pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}
in terms of the family
\boldsymbol{\bm{\gamma}}^{n-1}
as follows. We consider any element
t
of rank
2
, and any atom
s
such that
\widehat{0}\lessdot s\lessdot t
and
\mathrm{Ind}(\widehat{0},s)=i
, and analyze whether the labels of this chain of length
2
form a descent or an ascent. Recall that
\mathrm{des}_{s}(i)
counts the number of indices in the labels above any element covering
s
with label
i
, that gives rise to a descent, and let
j=\mathrm{Ind}(s,t)
.

•

If
j\leqslant\mathrm{des}(i)
, then
\lambda(\widehat{0},s)\vartriangleright\lambda(s,t)
, giving rise to a descent.

•

If
j>\mathrm{des}(i)
, then
\lambda(\widehat{0},s)\trianglelefteqslant\lambda(s,t)
, giving rise to an ascent.

To simplify notations we denote
\omega=\omega_{0}
. By rank-uniformity of
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
, we have the following recursion

\gamma^{n}_{i,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{4.97986pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}=\omega(i)\cdot\sum_{j>\mathrm{des}(i)}\gamma^{\,n-1}_{j}=\omega(i)\cdot\sum_{j>\mathrm{des}(i)}\gamma^{\,n-1}_{j}.

This recursion can be rewritten as

\boldsymbol{\bm{\gamma}}_{{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}^{n}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\boldsymbol{\bm{\gamma}}^{n-1},

where the matrix

is defined by

,

is an interlacing sequence of polynomials.
