{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{4.97986pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}+\gamma^{n}_{i,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.36769pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.09 L 6.69 0.39}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{0.14142pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
the result follows from statements
1
,
3
,
4
and convexity of interlacing polynomials (Proposition
2.6
).
∎

Proof of
5.1
.

The theorem follows from
Lemma

5.3
and
Proposition

5.2
.
∎

Our proof of
5.1
implies several interlacing relations among different Chow polynomials. In general, both the (
\gamma
-polynomials of the) Chow polynomials of
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{P}^{\ast}
are conjectured to interlace the (
\gamma
-polynomials of the) the augmented Chow polynomials for matroids
[
29
, Conjecture 5.7]
. Li also conjectured that the (
\gamma
-polynomial of the) Chow polynomial of a matroid contracted at an atom interlaces the (
\gamma
-polynomial of the) Chow polynomial of the matroid. See
Section

8
. The theorem below settles those conjectures for UMEL-shellable posets.

Theorem
1.3

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a UMEL-shellable poset. The following statements hold:

(1)

The roots of
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
and
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)
interlace.

(2)

For an atom
a
of
\mathrm{P}
, the roots of
\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]}(x)
and
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)

interlace.

(3)

For an atom
a
of
\mathrm{P}
, the roots of
\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]}(x)
and
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}}(x)

interlace.

The validity of the statement above is implied by the following theorem, by the fact that the
\gamma
-transformation preserves interlacing polynomials in
Lemma

2.12
. We also add a condition under which the Chow polynomial of a contraction at an atom interlaces the Chow polynomial of the poset.

Theorem 5.4

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a UMEL-shellable poset, and let
a
be an atom of
\mathrm{P}
. We have the following interlacing relations.

(1)

The
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};y)
interlaces the
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y)

\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};y)\preceq\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y).

(2)

The
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y)
interlaces the
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};y)

\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y)\preceq\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};y).

(3)

The
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y)
interlaces the
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y)

\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y)\preceq\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y).

Furthermore, if
\mathrm{des}_{0}(\ell)=\ell^{\prime}
, where
\ell
(resp.
\ell^{\prime}
) denotes the number of distinct labels above
\widehat{0}
(resp. any atom), then we have

(iv)

The
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y)
interlaces the
\gamma
-polynomial
\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};y)

\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y)\preceq\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};y).

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.09 L 6.69 0.39}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{0.14142pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\right),

which is the desired result by
Proposition

5.2
.

(2)

By Lemma
5.3
, the sequence
(\gamma^{n}_{1,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}},\ldots,\gamma^{n}_{\ell,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}})
is interlacing. By convexity of interlacing (Proposition
2.6
) we get

\gamma^{n}_{1,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{4.97986pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\preceq\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\gamma^{n}_{i,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{4.97986pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}=\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};y),

where the last equality comes from Proposition
5.2
. However, there cannot be a descent starting from the smallest label above
\widehat{0}
, as this would contradict the lexicographic minimality of the unique maximal chain with increasing labels. This implies the equality
\gamma^{n}_{1,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{4.97986pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}=\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y)
which gives the second statement.

(3)

By Lemma
5.3
, the sequence
(\gamma^{n}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{n}_{\ell})
is interlacing. By convexity of interlacing we obtain

\gamma^{n}_{1}\preceq\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\gamma^{n}_{i}=\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}};y).

However, by the same argument as in the preceding paragraph we have
\gamma^{n}_{1}=\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y)
which gives the result.

(4)

Let us assume
\mathrm{des}_{0}(\ell)=\ell^{\prime}.
This implies that we have
\gamma^{n}_{\ell}=y\,\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y)
. By Lemma
5.3
the sequence
(\gamma^{n}_{1,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{4.97986pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}},\ldots,\gamma^{n}_{\ell,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.7071}{4.97986pt}{4.97986pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}},\gamma^{n}_{\ell})
is interlacing, and so by convexity this implies

\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}\gamma^{n}_{i,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}=\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}};y)\preceq\gamma^{n}_{\ell}=y\,\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{[a,\widehat{1}]};y),

which is equivalent to the desired result. ∎

Note that the condition
\mathrm{des}_{0}(\ell)=\ell^{\prime}
, or, in other words, the condition that there is no ascent starting from the biggest label above
\widehat{0}
, does not have to be true in general, as Example
3.3
shows. However, that condition is always verified when
\mathrm{P}
is atomic. Indeed in that case, if
a
is an atom with maximal label then for all
a\lessdot t
in
\mathrm{P}
by atomicity of
\mathrm{P}
the element
t
is above another atom
a^{\prime}.
By definition of
a
we have
\lambda(\widehat{0},a^{\prime})\trianglelefteqslant\lambda(\widehat{0},a)
. If
\lambda(\widehat{0},a^{\prime})\vartriangleleft\lambda(\widehat{0},a)
then by minimality of the unique maximal chain with increasing labels between
\widehat{0}
and
t
we must have
\lambda(\widehat{0},a)\vartriangleright\lambda(a,t).
If
\lambda(\widehat{0},a^{\prime})=\lambda(\widehat{0},a)
, then we must also have
\lambda(\widehat{0},a)\vartriangleright\lambda(a,t).
Indeed, if the contrary was true, by the uniqueness of the maximal chain with increasing labels between
\widehat{0}
and
t
we would have
\lambda(\widehat{0},a^{\prime})\vartriangleright\lambda(a,t),
which would contradict the lexicographic minimality of that maximal chain.

6.
Rank-uniform supersolvable lattices

In this section, we explore the interactions between the notion of rank-uniform labeled posets introduced in this article and the theory of supersolvable lattices (see Definition
6.4
). The main result of this section shows that all rank-uniform supersolvable lattices are UMEL-shellable (Theorem
1.7
), and so we may specialize Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
5.1
to them. This parallels the classical result that characteristic polynomials of supersolvable geometric lattices are real-rooted (see
[
50
]
). We start with some preliminaries.

Definition 6.1

A finite poset
\mathrm{L}
is called a
lattice
if every pair of elements
s,t\in\mathrm{L}
has a unique supremum in
\mathrm{L}
(denoted
s\vee t
and called the
join
of
s
and
t
), and a unique infimum in
\mathrm{L}
(denoted
s\wedge t
and called the
meet
of
s
and
t
).

Definition 6.2

A pair
(s,t)
in a lattice
\mathrm{L}
is called
modular
if for all
u\leqslant t\in\mathrm{L}
we have the equality

u\vee(s\wedge t)=(u\vee s)\wedge t.

An element
s
in a lattice
\mathrm{L}
is called
modular
if for all
t\in\mathrm{L}
, both pairs
(s,t)
and
(t,s)
are modular. A finite lattice is
modular
if all its elements are modular.

Modular elements satisfy the following lemma, which we will need later.

Lemma 6.3

(
[
8
]
, Section IV.2)

For any elements
s,t
in some lattice
\mathrm{L}
, if
s
is modular then taking the join with
t
defines an isomorphism of posets from
[s\wedge t,s]
to
[t,s\vee t]
, with inverse given by taking the meet with
s
.

The following notion was introduced by Stanley in
[
49
]
.

Definition 6.4

A finite lattice
\mathrm{L}
is
supersolvable
if
\mathrm{L}
admits a maximal chain of modular elements.

Supersolvable lattices appear in several areas of mathematics. The motivation behind the terminology comes from the fact that this class of posets contains lattices of subgroups of finite supersolvable groups
[
49
]
. Furthermore, from the definition it is clear that modular lattices are supersolvable. For instance, the poset
\mathrm{P}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n})
consisting of all subspaces of the vector space
(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{n}
over the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
ordered by inclusion is modular and so it is supersolvable—the poset
\mathrm{P}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n})
is often called a
projective geometry
or a
vector space lattice
. Further examples of supersolvable lattices in other areas of combinatorics include the non-crossing partition lattices of type A and the class of shuffle posets (see
[
35
, Chapter 4]
).

In hyperplane arrangement theory, a flat
F
in the intersection lattice
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{H}}
of a complex hyperplane arrangment
\mathcal{H}
is modular if and only if the natural map
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{H}^{F}}
between the arrangement complement
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
of
\mathcal{H}
and the arrangement complement
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{H}^{F}}
of the localization
\mathcal{H}^{F}
is a fibration (see
[
45
]
for the proof of that result, and see
[
43
]
for a general reference on hyperplane arrangements). Thus, an intersection lattice
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{H}}
is supersolvable if and only if there is a tower of codimension
1
fibrations between
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
, induced by successive localizations. For instance, the intersection lattice of the
n
-th (type A) braid arrangement is supersolvable. Indeed the arrangement complement of the
n
-th braid arrangement is the
n
-th configuration space
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
of
\mathbb{C}
, and we have a tower of codimension
1
fibrations between
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
given by forgetting points one after the other. The intersection lattice of the
n
-th braid arrangement is isomorphic to the
n
-th partition lattice, which is simply the set of all set partitions of
[n]
ordered by refinement.

The following lemma shows that supersolvability is a hereditary property.

Lemma 6.5

(
[
49
, Proposition 3.2]
)

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a supersolvable lattice with maximal chain of modular elements
(m_{i})_{0\leqslant i\leqslant n}
. For any
s\in\mathrm{L}
, the set of elements

s\vee m\coloneqq\{s\vee m_{0},\ldots,s\vee m_{n}\}

forms a maximal chain of modular elements of the interval
[s,\widehat{1}],
and the set of elements

s\wedge m\coloneqq\{s\wedge m_{0},\ldots,s\wedge m_{n}\}

forms a maximal chain of modular elements of the interval
[\widehat{0},s]
.

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a supersolvable lattice with maximal chain of modular elements

\widehat{0}=m_{0}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot m_{n}=\widehat{1}.

As explained in
[
41
]
one can define an EL-labeling
\lambda_{m}
on
\mathrm{L}
by setting

\lambda_{m}:\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{L})\rightarrow[n],\quad\lambda_{m}(s,t)\coloneqq\min\{i\,|\,s\vee m_{i}\geqslant t\}.

We will call this labeling the
supersolvable labeling
associated to
\mathrm{L}
and the maximal chain
m
. We will need the two lemmas below about supersolvable labelings.

Lemma 6.6

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a supersolvable lattice with maximal chain of modular elements
(m_{i})_{0\leqslant i\leqslant n}
and associated supersolvable labeling
\lambda_{m}
. For every atom
s
of
\mathrm{L}
,

\mathrm{des}(\widehat{0},s)=\mathrm{Ind}(\widehat{0},s)-1.

Proof.

Denote by
\lambda_{1}\vartriangleleft\lambda_{2}\vartriangleleft\cdots
the labels of the atoms of
\mathrm{L}.
For all
i<\mathrm{Ind}(\widehat{0},s)
there exists an atom
s_{i}
of
\mathrm{L}
such that
\lambda_{m}(\widehat{0},s_{i})=\lambda_{i}
. By modularity of
m_{\lambda_{m}(\widehat{0},s)-1}
and Lemma
6.3
, taking the join with
s
defines an isomorphism from
[\widehat{0},m_{\lambda_{m}(\widehat{0},s)-1}]
to
[s,m_{\lambda_{m}(\widehat{0},s)-1}]
. This implies that for all
i<\mathrm{Ind}(\widehat{0},s)
the element
s\vee s_{i}
must cover
s
, and
s_{i}
is the unique element below
m_{\lambda_{m}(\widehat{0},s)-1}
such that its join with
s
is
s\vee s_{i}
. This implies that we have
\lambda_{m}(s,s\vee s_{i})=\lambda_{i}
. This proves that every label
\lambda_{i}
with
i<\mathrm{Ind}(\widehat{0},s)
appears as a label above
s
, and so we have the inequality
\mathrm{des}(\widehat{0},s)\geqslant\mathrm{Ind}(\widehat{0},s)-1.

For the converse inequality, if
s\lessdot t
is a covering relation such that
\lambda_{m}(s,t)\vartriangleleft\lambda_{m}(\widehat{0},s)
by the definition of the supersolvable labeling
\lambda_{m}
this means that
m_{\lambda_{m}(\widehat{0},s)-1}\vee s\geqslant t
and so by Lemma
6.3
we get the existence of an atom of
\mathrm{L}
having label
\lambda_{m}(s,t)
. This proves that every label appearing as a descent above
s
also appears as the label of an atom of
\mathrm{L}
and so we get
\mathrm{des}(\widehat{0},s)\leqslant\mathrm{Ind}(\widehat{0},s)-1.

∎

Lemma 6.7

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a supersolvable lattice with maximal chain of modular elements
(m_{i})_{0\leqslant i\leqslant n},
and let
s
be some element of
\mathrm{L}.
There exists a monotonic injection

\phi_{s}:[n-\operatorname{rk}s]\hookrightarrow[n]

such that for every covering relation
t\lessdot u\in[s,\widehat{1}]
we have the equality

\phi_{s}(\lambda_{s\vee m}(t,u))=\lambda_{m}(t,u).

In particular
([s,\widehat{1}],(\lambda_{m})_{s,\widehat{1}})
and
([s,\widehat{1}],\lambda_{s\vee m})
have the same width and descent numbers.

Proof.

Denote by
m^{s}_{0}=s\lessdot\cdots\lessdot\widehat{1}=m^{s}_{n-\operatorname{rk}s}
the elements of the set
s\vee m
(see Lemma
6.5
). For all
i\in[n-\operatorname{rk}s]
put
\phi_{s}(i)\coloneqq\min\{j\in[n]\,|\,m_{j}\vee s=m^{s}_{i}\}
. One can see that
\phi_{s}
is a monotonic injection from
[n-\operatorname{rk}(s)]
to
[n]
. Let us prove the equality
\lambda_{m}(t,u)=\phi_{s}(\lambda_{s\vee m}(t,u))
for all covering relation
t\lessdot u
in
[s,\widehat{1}]
, by proving the inequality in both direction.

Denote by
k
the integer such that
s\vee m_{\lambda_{m}(t,u)}=m^{s}_{k}
. Since
m^{x}_{k}\vee t=m_{\lambda(t,u)}\vee t\geqslant u,
we have
k\geqslant\lambda_{s\vee m}(t,u).
Furthermore we have
\phi_{s}(k)=\lambda_{m}(t,u),
because if we had
m_{\lambda(t,u)-1}\vee s=m^{s}_{k},
then we would have
m_{\lambda(t,u)-1}\vee t\geqslant u,
contradicting the definition of
\lambda_{m}(t,u).
By monotonicity of
\phi^{s}
this implies that we have
\lambda_{m}(t,u)=\phi^{s}(k)\trianglerighteqslant\phi^{s}(\lambda_{s\vee m}(t,u)).

On the other hand by definition we have
m^{s}_{\lambda_{s\vee m}(s,t)}\vee t\geqslant u,
which implies that we have
m_{\phi^{s}(\lambda_{s\vee m}(t,u))}\vee t\geqslant u
which implies that we have
\lambda_{m}(t,u)\trianglelefteqslant\phi^{s}(\lambda_{s\vee m}(t,u)).

∎

If
\mathrm{L}
is a supersolvable lattice with maximal chain of modular elements
(m_{i})_{0\leqslant i\leqslant n}
, notice that Lemma
6.3
implies that for any atom
s\gtrdot\widehat{0}
which is not below
m_{n-1}
we have an isomorphism of posets
[\widehat{0},m_{n-1}]\simeq[s,\widehat{1}]
given by taking the join with
s
, and this isomorphism preserves the induced labelings on both sides. This means that supersolvable lattices with their supersolvable labelings are at least partially rank-uniform, because for instance
\omega_{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}}}(i)
is constant on rank
1
elements not below
m_{n-1},
for all
i
. However, they do not have to be rank-uniform in general. The following theorem shows that for supersolvable lattices, in order to check lexicographic rank-uniformity one only needs to check classical rank-uniformity, and in that case the labeling is automatically monotonic. A special subclass of rank-uniform supersolvable geometric lattices was studied in detail in
[
21
]
.

Theorem
1.7

Every rank-uniform supersolvable lattice is UMEL-shellable.

Proof.

We first check the uniformity of widths. For all
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
let us denote by
\omega^{k}
the width in the labeled poset
([\widehat{0},m_{k}],\lambda_{m})
. Let us prove by induction on the rank
n
of
\mathrm{L}
that for any atom
s
of
\mathrm{L}
we have
\omega_{s}=\omega^{n-1}_{\widehat{0}}
. For
s\not\leqslant m_{n-1}
as explained in the previous paragraph this is a consequence of the isomorphism
[s,\widehat{1}]\simeq[\widehat{0},m_{n-1}]
given by Lemma
6.3
. That isomorphism also implies that
[\widehat{0},m_{n-1}]
is a rank-uniform lattice, which is also supersolvable by Lemma
6.5
. This means that by induction we can assume that we have the equality
\omega^{n-1}_{s}=\omega^{n-2}_{\widehat{0}}
for every atom
s
of
[\widehat{0},m_{n-1}].
Consequently, for every atom
s
of
\mathrm{L}
below
m_{n-1}
and every
i\leqslant n-2
we have

\omega_{s}(i)=\omega^{n-1}_{s}(i)=\omega^{n-2}_{\widehat{0}}(i)=\omega^{n-1}_{\widehat{0}}(i).

For every
i\geqslant n
we have
\omega_{s}(i)=0=\omega^{n-1}_{\widehat{0}}(i)
by Lemma
6.7
. By rank-uniformity every atom of
s
is covered by the same number of elements which is
\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1}\omega_{\widehat{0}}(i).
This implies that we must have
\omega_{s}(n-1)=\omega^{n-1}_{\widehat{0}}(n-1),
which concludes the proof. By a direct induction one gets that for all
s\in\mathrm{L}
one has the equality
\omega_{s}=\omega^{n-\operatorname{rk}(s)}_{\widehat{0}}
which gives the desired uniformity.

By Lemmas
6.6
and
6.7
, for all
s\lessdot t\in\mathrm{L}
one has
\mathrm{des}(s,t)=\mathrm{Ind}(s,t)-1
. This proves both the uniformity of the descent numbers of
(\mathrm{L},\lambda_{m})
and the monotonicity of
(\mathrm{L},\lambda_{m}).

∎

Corollary 6.8

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a rank-uniform supersolvable lattice. The
h
-polynomial of the order complex of
\mathrm{L}
, the Chow polynomials of
\mathrm{L}
and
\mathrm{L}^{\ast}
, and the augmented Chow polynomials of
\mathrm{L}
and
\mathrm{L}^{\ast}
are real-rooted.

Corollary 6.9

For every integer
n\geqslant 2
and every finite group
G
, the
h
-polynomial of the order complex of
\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})
, the Chow polynomial of
\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})
and the augmented Chow polynomial of
\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})
are real-rooted.

For the
h
-polynomial the statement already appeared in
[
14
, Section 4]
(see also
[
6
]
for the two special cases
G=\{0\},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
). For the Chow polynomial the result appeared concurrently and independently in
[
15
, Section 6]
using different methods.

Proof.

It was seen in Example
3.5
and proved by
[
23
, Theorem 4]
that Dowling geometries are supersolvable. By
[
23
, Theorem 2(a)]
, if
F\in\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})
has corank
r
then we have an isomorphism of posets
[F,\widehat{1}]\simeq\mathrm{Q}_{r}(\textrm{G}).
This shows that
\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})
is rank-uniform and we can conclude by Theorem
1.7
, Theorem
1.5
, and Theorem
5.1
.
∎

Example 6.10

(Chow polynomials for braid matroids of type
B_{n}
)

As explained in
Example

3.5
, when
\textrm{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the Chow polynomial
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\textrm{B}}_{n}(x)
is equal to the Chow polynomial
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\textrm{Q}_{n}(\textrm{G})}(x)
. We list the first few explicit
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\textrm{B}}_{n}(x)
, which only have real and nonpositive roots by our corollary above.

\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{n}^{\textrm{B}}(x)=\begin{cases}x^{2}+14x+1,&n=3,\\[4.0pt]
x^{3}+99x^{2}+99x+1,&n=4,\\[4.0pt]
x^{4}+622x^{3}+3162x^{2}+622x+1,&n=5,\\[4.0pt]
x^{5}+4051x^{4}+65812x^{3}+65812x^{2}+4051x+1,&n=6,\\[4.0pt]
x^{6}+28590x^{5}+1205199x^{4}+3724100x^{3}+1205199x^{2}+28590x+1,&n=7.\\[4.0pt]
\end{cases}

7.
Rank-selection of rank-uniform labeled posets

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a finite graded bounded poset of rank
n
. For any subset
S\subseteq[n-1]
one can consider the rank-selected subposet
\mathrm{P}_{S}
of
\mathrm{P}
at
S
, which is

\mathrm{P}_{S}\coloneqq\{s\in\mathrm{P}\,|\,\operatorname{rk}s\in S\sqcup\{0,n\}\}.

In the particular case when
S=[k]
for some
k
the poset
\mathrm{P}_{S}
will be called the
(n-k-1)
-th truncation of
\mathrm{P}
and will be denoted
\operatorname{Tr}^{n-k-1}(\mathrm{P})
. In this section we explore the interactions between the notion of labeled rank-uniform poset introduced in this article, and rank-selection. Our first result is the following.

Proposition 7.1

Let
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
be a rank-uniform labeled poset of rank
n
. For all
1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
the truncation
\operatorname{Tr}^{k}(\mathrm{P})
admits an EL-labeling
\lambda^{k}
such that
(\operatorname{Tr}^{k}(\mathrm{P}),\lambda^{k})
is a rank-uniform labeled poset. Furthermore, if
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is monotonic then so is
(\operatorname{Tr}^{k}(\mathrm{P}),\lambda^{k}).

Proof.

By induction it is enough to prove the result for
k=1
. In that case
\operatorname{Tr}^{1}(\mathrm{P})
is simply the poset
\mathrm{P}
with all the elements of rank
n-1
removed. We set
\lambda^{1}(s,t)=\lambda(s,t)
for all
s\lessdot t\in P
with
\operatorname{rk}t\leqslant n-2.
If
s\in\mathrm{P}
has rank
n-2
, then there exists a unique element
s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{P}
of rank
n-1
such that we have
\lambda(s,s^{\prime})\trianglelefteq\lambda(s^{\prime},\widehat{1}).
We then set
\lambda^{1}(s,\widehat{1})=\lambda(s,s^{\prime}).
One can check that
\lambda^{1}
is an EL-labeling of
\operatorname{Tr}^{1}(\mathrm{P})
. Let us check that
(\operatorname{Tr}^{1}(\mathrm{P}),\lambda^{1})
is rank-uniform. For the widths we only have to check uniformity at rank
n-2,
which is immediate since in
\operatorname{Tr}^{1}(\mathrm{P})
there is a unique covering relation above an element of rank
n-2
. For the descent numbers we only need to check uniformity at rank
n-3,n-2.
Let us denote by
\mathrm{des}^{1}
the descent function in
\operatorname{Tr}^{1}(\mathrm{P}).
For all
s\lessdot t\in\mathrm{P}
with
s
of rank
n-3
we have
\mathrm{des}^{1}(s,t)=1
when
\mathrm{des}_{n-3}(\mathrm{Ind}(s,t))>0
and
\mathrm{des}^{1}(s,t)=0
otherwise, which proves uniformity. This computation also shows that if
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is monotonic, then
\mathrm{des}_{n-3}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.6}{$\bullet$}}}})
is weakly-increasing, and so
\mathrm{des}^{1}_{n-3}(\bullet)
is too, which implies that
(\operatorname{Tr}^{1}(\mathrm{P}),\lambda^{1})
is monotonic as well.
∎

This has the following consequence.

Corollary 7.2

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a UMEL-shellable poset. The
h
-polynomial, the Chow polynomial and the augmented Chow polynomial of
\mathrm{P}^{\ast}
are real-rooted.

Proof.

For the
h
-polynomial it is immediate from the definitions that
h_{\mathrm{P}}=h_{\mathrm{P}^{\ast}}
and so the result follows from Theorem
1.5
. For the Chow polynomial, by Lemma
2.4
the result follows from Proposition
7.1
and Theorem
5.1
. For the augmented Chow polynomial it suffices to apply Lemma
2.4
and Theorem
5.1
.
∎

We were not able to prove or disprove that monotonic rank-uniform labeled posets are closed under general rank-selection, so we leave it as a question.

Question 7.3

Let
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
be a monotonic rank-uniform labeled poset of rank
n
, and let
S
be a subset of
[n-1]
. Does the rank selected subposet
\mathrm{P}_{S}
admit an EL-labeling
\lambda^{S}
such that
(\mathrm{P}_{S},\lambda^{S})
is a monotonic rank-uniform labeled poset?

By
[
39
, Theorem 4.1.1]
, if a ranked poset
\mathrm{P}
has an EL-labeling
\lambda
, then every rank-selected subposet
\mathrm{P}_{S}
admits an explicit EL-labeling
\lambda^{S}
. One can show that if
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is rank-uniform then so is
(\mathrm{P}_{S},\lambda^{S})
. However it is not true that the monotonicity of
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
implies that of
(\mathrm{P}_{S},\lambda^{S})
, as can be seen already for boolean lattices for instance. Nevertheless, it is still possible that one might be able to find another EL-labeling of
\mathrm{P}_{S}
which preserves monotonicity.

Despite this lack of result, we still have the following theorem, which generalizes Theorem
5.1
.

Theorem
1.6

Let
\mathrm{P}
be UMEL-shellable poset and let
\mathrm{P}_{S}
be a rank-selected subposet of
\mathrm{P}
. Then the following holds:

(1)

The Chow polynomials
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}}(x)
and
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}^{*}}(x)
have nonpositive real roots.

(2)

The augmented Chow polynomial
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}}(x)
has nonpositive real roots.

(3)

The roots of the polynomials
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}}(x)
and
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}}(x)
interlace.

(4)

The
h
-polynomial of the order complex
\Delta(\mathrm{P}_{S})
has nonpositive real roots.

The proof consists of a modification of the proof of
[
4
, Theorem 3.1]
, adding the same ideas that we have used for the proofs of Theorems
5.1
and
1.5
.

Proof.

We start with the statement on the
h
-polynomial. Let us denote by
h^{S}(y)
the
h
-polynomial of the order complex of
\mathrm{P}_{S}
. For any
s\in\mathrm{P}
and any integer
i\geqslant 1
we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{S,s}_{i}\coloneqq\{s=s_{0}\lessdot s_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{n-\operatorname{rk}(s)}=\widehat{1}\,|\,\mathrm{Des}(C)\subseteq S,\mathrm{Ind}(s_{0},s_{1})=i\}

and for every
s\in\mathrm{P}
and for all
S\subseteq[n-\operatorname{rk}(s)-1]
, we denote the sequence of polynomials

\mathbf{h}^{S,s}(y)\coloneqq\left(\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{S,s}_{i}}y^{\mathrm{des}(C)}\right)_{i\geqslant 1}.

Since

h^{S}(y)=\sum_{T\subseteq S}\beta(T)y^{|T|}=\sum_{i\geqslant 1}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{S,\widehat{0}}_{i}}y^{\mathrm{des}(C)}=\sum_{i\geq 1}h^{S,s}_{i}(y),

it is sufficient to prove that the sequence of polynomials
\mathbf{h}^{S,\widehat{0}}
is an interlacing sequence, which we prove by induction on the rank and the set
S
. By uniformity of
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
the polynomials
h_{i}^{S,s}(y)
only depend on the corank
k
of
s
, and so we write
h_{i}^{S,k}(y)
instead. For any
s
of corank
k
and any
S\subseteq[k-1]
, if
1\in S
then by partitioning the elements of
\mathcal{C}_{s,S}(i)
according to their second label we get the vectorial formula

\mathbf{h}^{S,k}(y)=\mathbf{A}(y)\cdot\mathbf{h}^{(S\setminus 1)-1,k-1}(y)

where
\mathbf{A}(y)=\begin{pmatrix}a_{ij}(y)\end{pmatrix}
is the matrix of polynomials defined by

a_{ij}(y)=\begin{cases}\omega_{n-k}(i)\>y&\text{ if }j\leqslant\mathrm{des}_{n-k}(i),\\
\omega_{n-k}(i)&\text{ if }j>\mathrm{des}_{n-k}(i).\end{cases}

By Lemma
2.9
the matrix
\mathbf{A}
satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
2.8
and so that theorem allows us to conclude by induction. On the other hand if
1\notin S
, we have the recursion formula

\mathbf{h}^{S,k}(y)=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{h}^{S-1,k-1}(y)

where
\mathbf{A}=(a_{ij})
is the matrix of real numbers defined by
a_{ij}(y)=0
if
j\leqslant\mathrm{des}_{n-k}(i)
and
a_{ij}(y)=\omega_{n-k}(i)
otherwise. One can check that the matrix
\mathbf{A}
satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
2.8
and so that theorem allows us to conclude by induction.

We prove together the statements on the augmented and non-augmented Chow polynomials of
\mathrm{P}_{S}
. By Lemma
2.11
it is enough to prove that
\gamma(\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}};y)
and
\gamma(\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{P}_{S}};y)
are real-rooted. For all
T\subseteq S
we say that
T
is
S
-stable if
T
does not contain two elements which are consecutive in
S.
For all
s\in\mathrm{P}
and all
i\geqslant 1
we introduce the following notations.

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{ndd},S,s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{C=\{s_{0}=s\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{n-\operatorname{rk}s}=\widehat{1}\}\,|\,\mathrm{des}(C)\subseteq S,\,\mathrm{des}(C)\,S\textrm{-stable}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{ndd},S,s}_{i}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{C=\{s_{0}=s\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{n-\operatorname{rk}s}=\widehat{1}\}\,|\,C\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{ndd},S,s},\mathrm{Ind}(s_{0},s_{1})=i\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{ndd},S,s}_{i,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{C=\{s_{0}=s\lessdot\cdots\lessdot s_{n-\operatorname{rk}s}=\widehat{1}\}\,|\,C\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{ndd},S,s},\mathrm{Ind}(s_{0},s_{1})=i,\min S\notin\mathrm{des}(C)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{ndd},S,s}_{i,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.36769pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

where the matrix
\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{ij}\end{pmatrix}
is defined by

a_{ij}=\begin{cases}\omega_{n-k}(i)&\text{ if }j>\mathrm{des}_{n-k}(i)\\
0&\text{ if }j\leqslant\mathrm{des}_{n-k}(i).\end{cases}

This gives the result by Theorem
2.8
.

By our inductive hypothesis the vector of polynomials in the right hand side is interlacing and we can conclude by Theorem
2.8
.

For
6
, if
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
notice that
\gamma^{S,k}_{i}=\gamma^{S,k}_{i,{\boldsymbol{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.36769pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

and so the result follows from

,

, and convexity of interlacing (see Proposition

).
∎

8.
Open questions

In this section, we formulate some additional open questions that we regard as relevant for further research.

8.1.
Geometric lattices

Recall that in Section
3
we proved that if an EL-labeled poset
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is rank-uniform then
\mathrm{P}
is rank-uniform (Lemma
3.6
). We feel it is important to ask ourselves to what extent the converse is true. More explicitly, if we are given a finite rank-uniform bounded poset
\mathrm{P}
, what conditions on
\mathrm{P}
would ensure that
\mathrm{P}
necessarily admits an EL-labeling
\lambda
such that
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is rank-uniform, or even monotonically rank-uniform? For instance Theorem
1.7
shows that supersolvability is one such condition. Obviously a necessary condition is that
\mathrm{P}
is EL-shellable. This leads us to the following question.

Question 8.1

Let
\mathrm{P}
be an EL-shellable poset. If
\mathrm{P}
is rank-uniform, does
\mathrm{P}
admit an EL-labeling
\lambda
such that
(\mathrm{P},\lambda)
is a (monotonic) EL-labeled poset?

Another property we feel may be interesting to look at is geometricity. A geometric lattice is the lattice of flats of a matroid (see
[
44
]
for a reference on that topic). Geometric lattices are relevant to our problem because they are known to have multiple EL-labelings, as the following classical result shows.

Proposition 8.2

(
[
10
]
)

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a geometric lattice and let
\vartriangleleft
be a total order on the set
\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{L})
of atoms of
\mathcal{L}
. The edge labeling
\lambda_{\vartriangleleft}:\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{L})\rightarrow(\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{L}),\vartriangleleft)
defined by

\lambda_{\vartriangleleft}(s\lessdot t)=\min\{u\in\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{L})\,|\,s\vee u=t\}

is an EL-labeling.

We also propose the following variant of Question
8.1
.

Question 8.3

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a geometric lattice. If
\mathcal{L}
is rank-uniform, does there exist a total order
\vartriangleleft
on
\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{L})
such that
(\mathcal{L},\lambda_{\vartriangleleft})
is a monotonic rank-uniform labeled poset?

This question seems already interesting for geometric lattices of rank
3
. If
\mathcal{L}
is a rank-uniform geometric lattice of rank
3
, then for any total order
\vartriangleleft
on
\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{L})
, the labeled poset
(\mathcal{L},\lambda_{\vartriangleleft})
is rank-uniform. Indeed, the uniformity at rank
2
is always satisfied. For any
s\in\mathcal{L}
of rank
1
and for all
i\geqslant 1
we have
\omega_{s}(i)=1
if
i\leqslant W_{1}(s)
and
\omega_{s}(i)=0
otherwise, which proves uniformity of width. For all
t\gtrdot s\in\mathcal{L}
we have
\mathrm{des}(s,t)=0
if
\mathrm{Ind}(s,t)=1
and
\mathrm{des}(s,t)=1
otherwise, which proves uniformity of descent. Only the question of monotonicity at rank 0 remains, which we reformulate below.

Question 8.4

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a geometric lattice of rank
3
. If
\mathcal{L}
is rank-uniform, does there exist a total order
\vartriangleleft
on
\mathrm{At}(\mathcal{L})
such that the quantity

\mathrm{des}_{0}(s)=|\{t\gtrdot s\,|\,\exists s^{\prime}\vartriangleleft s\textrm{ s.t. }s^{\prime}\leqslant t\}|

defined for all atoms
s
, is weakly increasing with respect to
\vartriangleleft
?

On the topic of interlacing, recall that Theorem
5.4
and the paragraph following it show that if
\mathcal{L}
is a UMEL-shellable geometric lattice, then for every atom
a\in\mathcal{L}
we have the interlacing relations

\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{[a,\widehat{1}]}\preceq\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathcal{L}},\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]}\preceq\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{L}}.

This motivates the following strengthening of Conjecture
1.1
.

Conjecture 8.5

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a geometric lattice, and let
a
be an atom of
\mathcal{L}
. The (augmented) Chow polynomial of the upper-interval
[a,\widehat{1}]
interlaces the (augmented) Chow polynomial of
\mathcal{L}
:

\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{[a,\widehat{1}]}\preceq\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathcal{L}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{H}_{[a,\widehat{1}]}\preceq\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{L}}.

In matroid terminology, the above conjecture is asserting that for any (simple) matroid
\mathrm{M}
on the ground set
E
, the contraction by a single element
i\in E
satisfies
\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{M}/i}\preceq\underline{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{M}}
and
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}/i}\preceq\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}
.

8.2.
TN-posets

Brändén and Saud-Maia-Leite
[
13
]
introduced the concept of a totally nonnegative poset (TN-poset for short). By definition, a TN-poset is a (lower) rank-uniform poset whose lower triangular matrix of (lower) Whitney numbers of the second kind
(W_{n-i}(j))_{i,j}
is totally nonnegative (meaning that all its minors are nonnegative).

In
[
14
]
and
[
15
]
the authors show that the chain polynomial and the Chow polynomial respectively of a TN-poset are real-rooted. Due to the similarity of the examples considered in those articles and the present work (Dowling lattices, projective geometries, uniform matroids, etc.) it is natural to inquire to what extent the notions of TN-posets and UMEL-shellable posets relate to each other. On that topic we propose the following conjecture.

Conjecture 8.6

Let
\mathrm{M}
be a matroid such that
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{M})
is a TN-poset. Then
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{M})
is UMEL-shellable.

The converse of the above conjecture is not true, because partition lattices are UMEL-shellable and they fail to be TN-posets. The conjecture is supported by the fact that TN-geometric lattices are in particular lower rank-uniform, and it is known that lower rank-uniform geometric lattices are upper rank-uniform (see
[
20
, Theorem 4.5]
).

A dual version of Conjecture
8.6
, which we propose as a question, would be the following.

Question 8.7

Let
\mathrm{M}
be a matroid such that
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{M})
is a UMEL-shellable poset. Is
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{M})^{*}
a TN-poset?

The above question is strongly related to a conjecture of Brändén and Saud-Maia-Leite
[
14
, Conjecture 4.3]
5
5

5

In the current arXiv posting of
[
14
]
, the conjecture contains a misprint. Brändén (personal communication) informed us that they conjecture that
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{M})^{*}
is a TN-poset whenever
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{M})
is rank-uniform.
.

We can ask analogous questions in the broader generality of graded bounded posets. However, since UMEL-shellable posets are EL-shellable, we need to restrict our attention to EL-shellable TN-posets (there exist TN-posets that are not Cohen–Macaulay). We ask the following questions.

Question 8.8

If
\mathrm{P}
is a UMEL-shellable poset, is
\mathrm{P}^{\ast}
a TN-poset? Conversely if
\mathrm{P}
is an EL-shellable TN-poset, is
\mathrm{P}^{\ast}
UMEL-shellable?

An affirmative answer to the first of the above two questions would be very interesting, as this would provide a combinatorial criterion for proving that a poset is TN.
